Drowsiness. A slight drowsiness is often noticed after a hearty meal, because active digestion draws a greater volume of blood to the stomach, so that the brain is relatively poorly supplied. In some southern countries this tendency is favoured and the siesta after the noon meal is a national cus- tom. Sometimes we hear of attacks of sleepiness occurring suddenly at certain periods of the day, or at irregular intervals. These are together abnormal, and in such cases there is almost always some poison at work in the nervous centres—usually a self-manufactured poison, which, because it is made in too great quantity, or because of disease cannot be eliminated rap- idly, accumulates in the system.

Child Labour. The greatest evil resulting from too long hours of labour is the want of sleep suffered by juvenile workers, many having been known to fall asleep in school over their lessons, and, fortunately, often been allowed to remain asleep by the kindly teachers, who happened to be aware of the cause of their fatigue. In addition to this shortage of sleep, there is the deprivation of recreation, and the thousand- and-one little pleasures which go, to make the happiness of a child, and which are rightly looked upon by the majority as the heritage of yonth.—" Agricultural Economist and Horticul- tural Review."

Fainting and Other Fits. A celebrated physician recently said: "I wish you would do something to teach people the difference between a fainting fit and epileptic fit. You see, a fainting fit is produced by the blood leaving the head. It is important to re- member this, because it determines the method of treating these afflicted people when no doctor is near, and because everyone occasienally finds it his duty to go to the help of some person in a fit, without a moment for reflection, let alone inquiry. If a person faints he ought to be laid down flat on his back, for this will facilitate the re- turn of blood to his head. Indeed, nature has provided for this, for a person who falirts will fall down and soon recover if no one interferes. On the other hand, a person with an epileptic or cataleptic fit, indicated by convulsions and frothing at the mouth, ought to be, proooefl so as to facilitate the flow of blood from the head to the lower parts of the body, which may be still further promoted by slapping the palms of the hands. Now, I cannot account for Vt, but the fact is that all ignorant people, in an emer- gency, reverse this treatment. A person who simply faints is carefully supported in a chair, and the face is plied with cold water applica- tions, fanned, the result being that the fainting is prolonged. On the other hand, a man who drops in the street with epilepsy or catalepsy is invariably laid flat on his back, which in some "cases is enough to till him."—" Family Doctor."

The Potato. A very powerful brandy is made of the humble potato. The potato but too often adulterates cocoa, potted,meats, honey, butter and tapioca. From potato leaves cigarettes are made, and from potato pulp, buttons, combs and pen- holders.

Onion and Tomato Salad. Take six ripe tomatoes and peel them, then cut in thin slices in a salad bowl. Grind or chop three medium sized onions, sprinkle the onions over the tomato slices, add salt, pepper, and vinegar. Garnish with lettuce, or cold boiled eggs, sliced.

Sliced Cucumbers. Remove thick slices from both ends and cut i off a thick paring, as the cucumber contains a bitter principle, a large quantity of which lies near the skin and stem end. Cut in very thin slices and keep in cold water until ready to serve. Drain, and cover with crushed ice for serving. Apple Betty. Two cupfuls of tart cooking apples, chopped, a cupful and a half of stale bread-crumbs, about four tablespoonfuls of sugar, one generous table- spoonful of butter, and the grated rind of one lemon and juice. Butter a pie-dish, divide the ingredients into four layers, beginning with bread-crumbs and finishing with-bread-crumbs. Sprinkle the sugar and lemon over the apples and cut the butter into tiny lumps and scatter over the crumbs. Bake' three-quarters of an hour in a moderate oven. Serve with ,cream sauce. Bread Sauce. A Boil two cupfuls of milk in a double boiler for thirty minutes with half a cup of fine white stale bread-crumbs, and one onion stuck with six cloves. Remove the onion and add half ,a tea- spoonful of salt. a little cayenne and two table- spoonfuls of butter.

Any vegetable puree can be prepared as fol- lows: Melt loz. of well-clarified dripping in a pan, and cook in it till tender, but without colouring, 40z. of onions, 2oz. of celery, and a bunch of herbs; then in five minutes lay in lib. of potatoes, carrots turnips, &c.' *according to what you wish to use, peeled and cut up, cover down the pan, and let it continue to cook for

A Lump of Humanity., She weighed 234 if she weighed an ounce, and she did weigh an ounce. The whole rink shook and rumbled as she struggled round in her efforts to master the whirling art. Suddenly—a terrific thud—a groan—arid there, upton the boarding, lay a heap of overbalanced femininity. The woman opened her eyes. " You will have to wait but a moment, madame," politely remarked a third. "We have just sent for the crane. I trust you are not hurt?" " N-n-n-no. I don't think so !" she gasped bravely back. "But, oh, there are somerdread- ful lumps in your floor!" "Lumps, be hanged, madame I" growled a half-smothered voice from underneath. "I'm not a lump; I'm one of the attendants !"

Mutual Compliments. At a certain church it is the pleasing custom at a marriage for the clergyman to kiss the bride after the ceremony. A young lady who was about to be married in the church did not relish the prospect, and instructed her prospec- tive husband when making arrangements to tell the clergyman that she did not wish him to kiss her. The bridegroom did as directed. "Well, George," said the young lady when he appeared, "did you tell the' clergyman that I did not wish him to kiss me?" "Oh, yes." "And what did he say?" 'He said that in' that case he would charge only half the usual fee.", Where Babies Are Used as Bait. Wot do ye think," said the sailor, " of nein! live babies for bait? We done it in Ceylon." "Babies for bait? Fishing for shark?" " No, crocodile. Baby bait is the only thing for crocodile, and everybody uses it. Ye rent baby down there for two shillings a day. " Of course," the sailor went on, " the thing ain't as cruel as it sounds. No harm ever comes to the babies, or else, o' course, their mothers wouldn't rent 'em. The kid is simply set on ,the soft mud bank of a crocodile stream, and the hunter lies hid near them, a sure pertec- tion. "The crocodile is lazy. He basks in the sun in mid-stream. Nothin' will draw him in to 'shore where we can pot him. But set a little, 'fat naked baby on the bank and tlie crocodile soon rouses up. In he comes, a greedy look in his dull eyes, and then we open fire. 'I have got as many as four crocodiles with one baby in a morning's fishin'. Some Cingalese women wot lives near good crocodile streams make as much as eight shillings a week out o' rentin' their babies for crocodile bait."

A BIG BELL. There ate 33 bells in Antwerp Cathedral, in Belgium, near which the Germans and the brave little Belgians have been fighting. The largest bell is seven feet wide and eight feet high.

• As a youth he attended the Military School at WoolwiCh, and when 21 years of age he joined the RoydrEngineers. Those who study hard and know their work thoroughly re bound to get on, and Lord Kitchener being a man of this , kind Was soon Promoted. In the war with the Boers he went to South AfriCa as ,Lord Roberts's chief officer, and later took charge. When the Boers asked for peace he acted with firmness and tact on behalf of our Government, and 'with fairziesa towards his late. enemies. • Now be has been appointed the Secretary of State for Wart. He likes all his orders obeyed at once. The story is told that when the British were fighting in the Soudan he gave an officer instructions to go across-a certain tract oY land. The officer went, but 'found a big clamp of bushes was in the way. He sent word to Lord Kitchener, who replied, "Cut the hush down!" This shows that he does not let 'Small things spoil his plans. There is another story told about him with reference to the South African, War. Before Lord Roberts left Cape Town he sent fora cer- tain Coionel and got- him to promise that he would "try" to perform a task in a fortnight. As the Colonel was leaving Lord Roberts's office he met Kitchener, who asked what had been decided. "Well," said the Colonel, "I have promised to do it in a fortnight." , "Now, Colonel," replied Lord Kitchener, "if this is not done within a week we shall have to see about sending you home." The Colonel did it in a week! Lord Kitchener is over six feet high, and al- though he is getting-on in years, he stands as erect as a young man:

orphans. Large quantities of Christmas cards come over from Germany, in sheets or in bulk. They are made up and finished in this country, and bear no indication of their place of origin. A good deal of the better class Christmas trade, both in toys and cards, has been captured in recent years by, Britisli manufacturers, but practically all of the cheap and gaudy kind is , still foreign. All sorts of devices will be resorted to in order to defeat the boycott of "Made in Germany" goods, but the public should insist upon guarantees from retailers, and deal severely with any who try to deceive purchasers.

The Wider Skirt. One of the new autumn styles will be wel- comed by women unable to wear very hard and severe lines. This type of suit is in the nature of a gown. It is cut quite straight, the upper part being very loose, and the hips swathed with a sash or band. The tunic is long and pleated, and flares out. The skirt is narrow and slit in several places to give greater free- doin of movement. Skirts of all kinds are wider, and in every way an improvement on their ultra narrow predecessors. But a very , wide short skirt takes from the height of its wearer, and is decidedly uncomfortable. It is, therefore, to be hoped that designers will be guided by a certain amount of reason and corn- mon-senset and, not rush to extremes in this matter. At present the new models are pleas- ing, graceful and only moderately wide. Taffetas is holding its own still for smart wear, and the best qualities are made in Yorkshire end the Midlands. The war has caused the manufac- ture of all British-made cloths, tweeds, serges, friezes and silks to receive quite an impetus, that will, it is hoped, be maintained.

Sleeves for the Autumn. Long sleeves are seen on all blouses and bodices intended for morning and afternoon wear, perhaps because long gloves are expen- sive. They often fall half over the hand. Among novelties for the itutumn is the high Gladstone collar. It is wor'n straight to the chin, where it turns out in two points, but it is only modcr- atelY stiff, just enough to keep it from flopping. Turn-back sailor collars in foulard, with a tiny piped edging of white, are charming. Linens, when striped, Make very pretty and serviceable collars and cuffs, because they wash and get up well. In muslin, too, of a specially strong make,

these sets are most useful and look exceedingly well when finished with a picot edge, hem- stitching, ,or the new beading, which looks like picot, but is put on separately.

Monkey Fur, and Fur Cloths. A very curious fur, made from the skin of the monkey, is being used,on dresses and other gar- ments as edgings, probably because its wiriness gives it a slight resemblance to fringe. The new fur coats are very attractive,' although a complete contrast to those of last year, for they are very full round the skirts'. The pelts that adapt themselves best to these styles are pony, natural musquash, coney, and, of course, seal. Fitch and Polinski look extremely handsOme. The fur cloths that proved such excellent imita- tions of expensive peltry last winter, will be used to a far greateilextent this-year. In broad- tail, seal plush and moleskin cloth, and more especially the latter, these cloths are particu- larly excellent. Velveteen coats will be in great demand. This winter's material is exceptionally handsome in appearance, and will be seen in black and dark brown. Coats have bold, big collars, and are generally double-breasted, and, with their handsome linings, are very desirable garments. Fur cloths will also be used for cavalier and other capes.

An Adfiptable Hat. A hat provided with several sets of trimmings is not a new idea, but to revive it just now when every woman is anxious to spend as little as possible on dress, in order to have more to give to the many war funds, is sensible. A model of this type was rather large, with a straight wide brim, lined with black. The crown was flat and straight, and two sets of trimming consisted of flowers, the third of tulle frills, which turned over and fell on the brim. Two huge pansies finished this mount. The flowers were in each case mounted on a band of silk, fastened under a big bow of ribbon. One consisted of a wreath of big roses, the other of small blossoms, with which were mingled grasses. The colours chosen for each trimming matched the dress with which it was worn. The French sailor hat is one of the most popular shapes at present, and is made in velvet, straw, and silk. The bridle, in black velvet, fastened with a silk rose, is seen on some 'hats. A new trimming is formed from the long lance-like leaf of the reed, and used chiefly on black hats.

HOME NOTES. Recipes and Useful Suggestions.

A Woman's Garden. It is a difficult question to solve as to what form the woman's garden should take. Every woman will, if she be a true gardener, give a touch of individuality to her own plot. For my part I vote for the moraine. It has many ad- vantages; once made it needs very little weed- ing, it can be filled with dainty, inexpensive, wee darlings' that count for nothing in the larger spaces of the garden, and yet can carry ' a thrill of joy to their possessor. Each individual plant becomes an intimate friend, we watch its gradual development, guard it from its enemies, glory in its fairy. loveliness, mourn over its untimely death, and so enjoy a real friendship with each specimen.—" Agricultural Economist and Horticultural Review."

Ostrich Eggs. An ostrich egg will Make an omelet for a dozen people. It is said that the eggs are very palatable, but are too valuable to be used as food.

HERE AND THERE.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

The Anxieties of the Queen of the Belgians. I The sympathy of the nation goes out to the Queen of the Belgians, who recently came over in order to place her children in safety in this country. Her Majesty is a Bavarian Princess, and it must add to her sorrows to know that her countrymen have so ruthlessly devastated her adopted country. Her anxieties for her children have always been acute, for Belgium was for years the resort of desperate anarchists, who, it was known, had plotted, during the late King's reign, to kill the sons of his heir. Con- sequently 'the two young Princes were never allowed to go out together. Of late years, how- ever, there has been no necessity to tae any. extraordinary precautions.

The Daughters of Canada. The Imperial daughters of the Empire in Canada areo-Pting up to their name in the most practical and splendid way. They ure fitting out a hospital ,hip for the wounded, and sending it on its errand of mercy with all possible speed. In this country the number of women of the wealthier classes who since the war began have evinced a desire to work for the Red Cross Societies is absolutely overwhelming. So great is the rush also to military and naval hospitals that our civil hospitals are in danger of suffer- ing from a shortage of nurses. Women with a bent for medicine should take it up now as a profession, for there will be a great opening for them as medical practitioners later on, since the war has very considerably depleted the number of available ,male doctors.

Pimples. Great benefit in the treatment of large red pimples that come on the face and shoulders 15 derived from washing the face and shoulders night and morning with strong salt and water, a tablespoonful of table salt to a teacupful of soft water. Live on plain, wholesome, nourish- ing food; eat plenty of fruit especially oranges and take a great deal of outdoor exercise. Sea bathing is very beneficial.

Headache. Bathing the -head behind the ears with hot water will often cure an obstinate headache.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 19,1914. 1,11 FARM AND GARDEN. 7GI7LDRir'S"x CORNER. 5 Conducted by UNCLE NED. k)=2.4.2Av0-oto=9-0vek9,40-a,OW. A

MY DEAR CpILDREN,— You will have noticed that last week I took Uncle Jack's place. I am claiming all of you now as my nephews and ni,ces, and twant you to be as loyal as you were to Uncle Jack. It is to be hoped you have tried to follow my advice as to how you could help England during and after the war. This week I want to write to you about per- severance. If you are told to do something that is very hard, keep on trying until you have done it—that's perseverance. You must have plenty of grit. Below I give a short account of Lord Kitchener, which shows what grit is. "Nothing is impossible in this world," says Alderman Peacock, the mayor of our own town. When a little boy he went to an elementary school, just like a lot of you do, and now he is the Mayor. How has he done it? By hard work, and not by only dreaming about doing some- thing. , No doobt lie has had many troubles, but he has mastered them.. He has lookAd for op- portunity and, not waited for opportunity to look for him. ' Perhaps you have heard the story about two , boys, Dick and Jack, who both went to'a school-, not very far from Warrington, and sat together I in the same class. Dick worked hard with, his lessons, but Jack did not. ' Now Dick is a mana- ger of a big works, whilst his old schoolmate is one among the hundreds of labourers employed by the firm. Does not this show how necessary it is to try to conquer? Forget the sentence "I cannot do it." Every- thing can be done if you will but try, and I want you to remember this. • I shall always be pleased to hear from any of my children. If you have, any friends who do not belong to our Corner,,ask them to send in their names. I will heartily welcome them. Again I Must ask you not to forget the com- petition—" Why I should be loyal "—which closes next week. I would like a lot more entries than I have so far received. Your loving UNCLE NED.

GOOD CROPS WILL AID GREAT BRITAIN. Nature has favoured us this year in the matter of crops—a vital consideration in such a time ef war and prospective trouble. Wheat, potatoes, beans and hops will give us a plenteous store, while barley is above' the average of, the last ten years, and the worst of our cereal crops, oats, is only short by one per cent. of that _average. Hops, which are nearly 20 per cent. better than last year, will make some amends for the shortage by the loss aris- ing from, the fact, that we import much of our needs of this commadity usually from Austria and Germany. Wheat is 6 per cent. better than the ten years' average, beans are nearly seven per cent. better, potatoes five per cent. Drought in many places has had an adverse effect upon the root crops, but harvesting bene- fited by the fine weather. The combined result of a review of the whole situation made by the "Times" is that, taking all crops into account, must be considered as highly satisfactory—the more so because of the exceedingly favourable weather during harvest time. It is true there have been interruptions and delays, even in districts which had before suffered from want of rain; but as a rule the harvest has been early and rapid, with the re- sult that there have been 'extraordinary 'crops of wheat, beans, potatoes, and hops; average crops of ,barley and oats; deficient yields of peas and mangels. Fruit crops, too, would appear to be particularly good. The supply of grass varies with the rainfall. As to the weakest link in the chain (the root crop) there is yet time for re- covery, as it is impossible to speak with conli- . dence until the close of autumn.

LORD KITCHENER.

On many walls and boards in Warrington yOu can see posters saying "Join Kitchener's Army," but how many of you have troubled to find out anything about the man whose name is printed on those papers? For instance, how .many of you know that Lord Kitchener is over 60 years of age and that he is very strict? Every boy and girl should know such‘thinge jas these, espe- cially now that this great man is looking after the welfare of our soldiers, our country, and ' our Empire. He was born in 1850, so that his 'exact ago is 64. He has seen a lot of battles in the Soudan and South Africa, and has more than once covered himself with,glorY. - •

THE BROKEN BRIDGE.

.. - Young people have 'often taken a prominent ' part on battlefields and proved of great help to their own country. During the war between the French and the Tyrolese, a people who live in the north-east of Italy on the bank of the River Ard, the French soldiers wanted to reach a certain village, but this could only be done by crossing a 'river which ran very. swiftly at the bottom of a deep ravine. The only means of crossing from one side to the other .was by 'a large tree which had fallen across the deep cut- ting and iested on both banks. To stop the French getting into the village it was necessary to destroy this natural bridge. Three hundred , Tyrolese men and a boy all crowded round their end of the tree, and, with the aid of axes, were soon • busily chopping it down. • , Whilst they were working, the French soldiers came along, and commenced shooting. so that one by one the men who were -breaking the bridge were killed. By the time the last man had sacrificed his life the task was nearly com- pleted. There was only little Albert Speck- bacher left, He took the place of his father, who was amongst the killed, and, with axe in hand, he faced the bullets and continued • the work of, smashing the bridge. When it was nearly breaking he jumped on to it and snapped the thin piece of wood that was still holding it together. The brave boy fell down into the river and was drowned, but the enemies of his country his body, buried it with honour. They also set his his gallant act, and, recovering up a monument to-tell how-bravely he had given up his life. This shows that all the brave deeds are not done by men and women.

BRITISH GENEROSITY. The British people have always answered the cry of the needy when great mine disasters have robbed wives and children of their breadwin- ners. Another instance has been given by the noble response to the Prince of Wales' appeal for the dependents of the soldiers. For this fund it only took 22 working days to raise the enormous sum of £2,000,000. The figure is more. striking if we take each working day as com- prising eight hours, for, then, it is estimated, that the money has been raised at the rate of .2190 every minute.

PERSEVERE By Jessie Simm. Though the road be long and dreary, And the skies be dull and grey; . Though you grow depressed and weary—

_ Home and friends seem far away; Waste no time in idle sighing,

_ Time thus spent is spent in vain; Don't despair, but keep on trying, Fortune yet will smile again.

Never mind a slip or stumble; Trials must be bravely borne. Let a good-for-nothing grumble, Let a coward look forlorn. Difficulties must be vanquished, Never dream of giving in; Summon up your failing courage, Go straight forward. You will win!

Just what immediate effect the European War will have on the meat situation in this country is problematical,, but the general opinion pre- vails in packing circles that if the "scrap" becomes general, as it promises to do, there will be an unprecedented demand on Uncle Sam for Provisions. War means waste, and also in the case of Europe, the diversion of thousands from the ranks of agriculture to' the ranks of the soldier. We will be expected to fill the demand for food-stuffs, as there is no other cupboard to go to that is quite so handy. Everybody recog- nises that there will be a tremendous call for salt and pickled meats, and probably more than the ciiuntry can supply, providing there are means of getting it over to them. Amen-can mutton, of course, is not an exportabk article, and there is no chance to send it to feed any of Europe's contending arniies unless we freeze it ii la Australia. But it is not necessary to export mutton in times like these to have the market boosted. The fact that much of the pork and beef can be sent abroad will make a bigger hole for the mutton to fill domestically, and the price of mutton will surely seek its natural level. If the big Powers decide to fight to the survival of the strongest it is morally certain that meat values will reach a level in this coun- try never before attained. The producers will benefit by this condition, and sheep raisers with both wool and mutton to sell will be doubly blessed. A big war will spread a famine over 'Europe such as was never known before, and America will have to furnish the bill of fare for a long time to come. If you are in the sheep business, double up; and if you are not in, bet- ter get in on as big a scale as possible without further delay. The millennium is on the-way for the meat producer.—" American Sheep Breeder."

HEDGE BRUSHING.

Now that the harvest is drawing to a cicse the hedges will soon be receiving our attention. A few remarks on hedge brushing may therefore not be out of place. The grass, fogs, &c., should first be mown out of the hedge bottom; any old scythe will be found good enough for this purpose. The main idea in brushing a hedge is to cut right down to ,stump of the-last year's growth, not leaving 3in. or 4in. of stubble, as is very often the cam,* The strokes should always be in an upward direction, otherwise the hedge will not have a smooth appearance when finished. When siding up, the 'last stroke before topping should be given a slight inward turn. This will prevent any ragged edges. I prefer to use a splasher with a hooked blade to a straight one, as the former does not allow the hedge to slip from the cut. The, hrushings should be raked up as soon as possible before the grass has grown through them, and either burned, or, what is better, used as a cOvering for the mangel clamp, for which purpose they will be found excellent.- _ _

An untidy hedge will spoil the looks of a well: ploughed field, Well-kept hedges are the hall- mark of good farming.—Clifton, in " Farm and Home."

AMERICAN HOPES OF A. MILLENIUM.

IN THE GARDEN.

' Enlarge the Area of Cultivation. ,

The Board of Agriculture are sending out numerous leaflets instructing the people what to grow and how to grow various crops to help to feed themselves during the war. In special leaflet No. 1 they give advice as to the bringing into cultivation of lands that are in ordinary times neglected, but may be made of incalcul- able value.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
GRASS LAND FOR THE SPADE. •

Many acres of grass -In-nd,..e;p;cially in the neighbourhood of large towns, might, we are told, be utilised for a great extension of allot- ments and other holdings under spadis cultiva- tion.

In the first place the land should be bastard trenched. To begin with the turf should be skimmed off in a thin layer two or three inches in depth from a trench which should be eigh- teen inches to two feet broad. The first spit of soil immediately below the turf should be re- moved. The bottom soil should then be stirred to a depth of six inches with a pick, digging fork or spade according to the nature of the sub- soil. The turf layer from the next trench should then be laid upside down on the stirred bottom of the first trench and the first spit of soil placed above it. This process should be continued till all the land has been dug over, the turf and first spit of soil from the first trench being used to level up the last. Land of this character is often very fertile and should give a good yield of potatoes or other planted crop the following summer. It can, however, seldom be used for crops raised from small seeds in the same year as it is broken up, for two reasons—(l) unless the turf is very good and free from weeds the result of the cultivation may bring up and favour the growth of a num- ber of weeds which have hitherto been kept under. A good deal of hoeing may be necessary to keep these down and this may interfere with the seedlings; (2) old grass land is often infested with wireworms (the young of the click beetle) and leather jackets ,(the young of the daddy long legs). These insects may 'completely destroy the crop as' soon as it begins to grow. Treatment for these pests is given in leaflets 10 and 11, but as the methods advised mat not be available at the present time the land should be frequently hoed and the weeds kept down so as to deprive the insects of their food for some months. Lime may also be worked into the land. . Advice as to the utilisation of derelict land is also given. We shall deal with this next week. Committees undertaking the organisation of the cultivation of such land may obtain expert advice at Agricultural Colleges or from the In- structors employed by County Councils. Copies of these leaflets and other leaflets de- scribing many of the common insect and fungus t pests may be obtained on application t 1 Board of Agriculture, 4, Whitehall-place, Lon- don, S.W., who are also prepared to give advice as to treatment in cases of special difficulty.

• • FACTS ABOUT FROSTS. During the last few years the Director of the Meteorological Office, Dr. W. N. Shaw, has given a great deal of attention to the question of spring frosts. These are, of course, more dan- gerous to the market gardener and fruit grower than any other phase of weather with which we are vilited. It has been established that bad frosts are likely to occur with the following con- ditions : In April, if the wind is in the north, and the barometer falls sharply, snow and sleet 'are highly 'probable, followed by a severe frost at night. At any time in the spring, especially after a spell of south-west wind with rain, frost is very likely to occur should the wind veer to "the north-west or west and the sky clear. Finally, a dangerous type of weather, even right through May, is that in which the sky is very clear with the wind in a northerly direction. However warm and sunny the day may be, the temperature falls very rapidly at night, and keen frost is almost certain.—" Agricultural Economist and Horticultural Review."

===== 0002601_19140919_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CYCLISTS' NOTEBOOK.

A Delightful Autumn Occupation

for Wheelmen.

MAKING.A TARRED ROAD.

Not very long ago I made a short inventory of the blessings the cyclist owes to the motor car. One of the other things we owe to that machine is the constant deterioration in the main roads, especially those used by the heavier motbr traffic. I have had to cast a back cover this Week after only six months' riding and 2,000 miles. It was riddled with chips of granite and spar, received during that August ride to Wales. On leaving Chester we both expected the Wrex- ham-road to furnish a score of miles of good riding. To our disappointment, it was just being re-tarred, and every two miles was an ironical notice, "Road under repair" (4s if we couldn't see!) "Cyclists are advised to walk." We walked a couple of miles and then risked it. Those chips that did not penetrate the tyres flew up on my stockings and my wife's dress, and we lost ever so much time picking them off, and then repairing two punctures they had already made in my still young tyre. But I do not envy the road surveyor his place. Great motor omnibuses thunder continuously along the road from Chester to Wrexham, and the tarred macadam is piled up in great waves eight or ten inches high. From Corwen to Cerrig-y- Druidion a traction engine with two monster trailer waggons has made that once fine road a morass, with puddle and ruts a foot deep. The last time we passed over the Dolgelly—Cross Foxes high road every step had to be walked— to ride a cycle was impossible. And every War- rington cyclist knows. the state of our own Man- chester-road a few miles out during the greater part of this spring and summer. The only remedy seems to be to limit by slaw the,weight and speed of these great engines. And it is quite time one piece of legislative prohibition was passed, namely, that no trailing cars what- ever shall be used on the road. Apart from the weight and the unwieldiness, especially at corners, the temptation to the reckless boy to ride on the connecting-rods is great. He jumps on whilst it is still, and when, at full speed, he hears his mates shout "Eh, th' driver's comin'," or realises that he is being taken far away from home, off he junjaps. Only this year a young friend of mine 'did this; be fell sideways, and both wheels of the trailer went over his--clog! A miracle thus saved his foot from being hope- lessly crushed.

A MACCLESFIELD CURIOSITY.

It is not every park that possesses a boulder forty tons in weight. This huge pebble was found near the town, and has since been placed in the park, where it is an object of interest to hundreds of people every year. The peculiarity with regard to it is that it differs from all the surrounding geological formations, and is of a structure identical with the granite rocks found at Ravensglass on the Cumberland coast. It is supposed to have been dropped by an iceberg and deposited on the floor of the prehistoric sea which many thousands of years ago must have covered this part of Cheshire.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
BLACKBERRIES. '

It is many years since such a crop of this delicious fruit crowned our Cheshire hedges. And the cycle is the ideal agent for placing you in the middle of the unplucked fruit. If you want to return with a load, make for any of the side lanes off the 'Arley-road; try the Cuddington by-lanes; Tabley abounds in loaded brambles. The highways through Delamere, the hedges at Aston by Sutton, and down at Pickering Bridge, or along the Weaver valley. A journey by cycle will show the futility of seeking fruit within three or four miles of the town, for dozens of pickers will be met, as they return, or over- taken, in the midst of their blue-black, happy, prickly work.

IRLD'S BAND AT BELLE VUE

FOOD AND POPULATION.

Dr. Manson and the Earth's Limitations.

CLASHING OF THEORIES.

On Sunday morning, at the Academy-street Men's Adult School, Dr. Manson continued his discussion on "Food and Population" from Sunday, August 30th, and the speaker summar- ised his remarks of that date. He reiterated the consequences that would follow if the population of the higher races declined in relation to that of the lower races, and showed that this in time would bring about the extinction of civilisation, as we now know it. On the other hand, if the higher races continue to increase in numbers, then the earth's limitations in p;pviding sub- sistence will tend to racial struggles for the elementary necessities of life. Without referring to the present war, the speaker pointed out that signs of these racial struggles were not wanting at the present day, and the attitude of the United States of America and our Colonies to Asiatic peoples showed this tendency. Malthus, more than a century ago, pointed out that "Man is necessarily confined in rooms"; "there are many parts of the globe

indeed hitherto uncultivated - and almost.- un- occupied, but the right of exterminating or driving into a corner where they must starve even the inhabitants of these thinly occupied regions will be questioned in a moral point of view."

Bernhardi, a couple of years ago, in his " Germany and the Next War," stated that: "Strong, healthy and flourishing nations in- crease in numbers. From a given molent they require a continual expansion of their frontiers, they require new territory for the accommoda- tion of their surplus population. Since almost every part of the globe is inhabited, new terri- tory must, as a rule, be obtained at the cost of its possessions—that is to say, by ,conquest, which thus bermes a law of necessity." Malthus thus differs from Bernhardi in ques- tioning the moral right of conquest, and gives his own remedy—the preventive increase of populations—as a means of mitigating the miseries of war and conflict.

To Bernhardi this remedy amounts to defeat and ultimate extinction, and he boldly pro- claims that " might is right," based on the necessity of survival—the survival of the fittest.

These' two views represent the apparent antagopism of Christian ethics and biological necessity, and the question, arises—Are they reconcilable? An interesting discussion followed.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
PROGRESSIVE REVELATION.

At the Sankey Bridges Men's Adult School on Sunday morning the subject a "Progressive revelation " was introduced for discussion in a short paper by a member of the s'chool. The speaker said the Bible, as we have it now, is ,the beginning of the growth of a religion on the Eastern shores of the Mediterranean. As time marches on we see how the idea of God, which has its origin in that of tribal God, per- sists and develops as the people emerge from a state of savagery; as they acquire laws and in- stitutions their conception of God expands. At first, He lives afar, but gradually through the prophets He comes to demand not sacrifices but personal hBliness, until in some of the utter- ances of the late prophets we have messages which come down into even this 20th century with freshness and a never-failing appeal. It was only after many painful and complex ex- periences that the religion of Israel was puri- fied from the grosser elements of heathenism which had been absorbed into it. The great turning point came with the preaching of John the Baptist, who, dissatisfied with the perform- ance of the ceremonials of the Temple, went forth into the desert to think out the problems which were surging in his mind. While there the vision of the incompleteness of the old teaching were borne on his mind. He declared that someone would come after him with a fuller and ampler message. Then came Jesus on the scene with His new conception of the union pf God and men, with the great supreme revelation—which is the very core of Christian- ity—that "1 and the Father are one." Jesus took this phrase, a vessel of light and filled it to the uttermost with that clear vision, that reality, which has brought man nearer to the spiritual conception of God as nothing else has done. An interesting discussion followed on the ap- parent antagonism of this view of revelation and the older idea of prophecy.

DANDRUFF ON HEAD.

28, Orde Hall-street, Bloomsbury, London, England.—" The complaint first started through scurf and dandruff deposited in my hair. My head soon became a mass of sores and eruption which was attended by constant irrita- tion and a burning sensation. It caused my hair to fall out and meant many sleepless nights owing to a terrible itching and burning sensation. I suffered in this way for over three years and tried various remedies without success. I was then recommended to try the Cuticura soap and Ointment, which I did. STOLE A COLLECTING BOX.

South Lancashire Private Bound Over.

TOO DRUNK TO KNOW.

A private in the South Lancashire Regiment, named Daniel Larkin, appeared before Mr. R. T. Fairclough (in the chair) and other magis- trates at the Borough Court on Saturday on a charge of stealing a Territorial collecting box, containing._ 2s. 30.

Supt. Williams-on said the prisoner was before the Court on Thursday, when he was remanded. He took the box from the shop of Mrs. Whit- taker, 9, Pinners-brow, about seven o'clock on the previous Wednesday morning. A number of boxes had been deposited in various buildings under the supervision of Mr. J. H. Smethurst, for the purpose of raising money for the local Territorials. The one which prisoner took was glued to the counter. Mrs. Whittaker left the box safe at 6.30 a.m., but when she returned some time later the box had been taken. A man drew the attention of a soldier on police duty to the prisoner, who had jumped on the box to open it, and had gone into a public-house. The soldier detained the prisoner, who was drunk, until the arrival of P.C. Dawson. When pris- oner had sobered down he was charged, and, in reply, saiddhe had nothing to say. , Prisoner said he came to Warrington to join the South Lancashires. :le had slept in the Barracks and had gone out early and got tbat drunk that he did pot know what he was doing. He would "rather be shot than sent to prison with a stain on his brow:" He was bound over for three months.

OFFICIALLY GUARANTEED REPORTS.

Study of a German Gentleman going into action,

nesses, had been struck several times with bayonets, and then, while still alive, the Ger- mans had poured petroleum over him, and thrown him into a house to which they set fire. A woman who came out of her house was killed in the same way. . . .

A witness, whose evidence has been taken by a reliable British subject, declares that he saw on August 26, not far from Malines during the last Belgian attack, an old man tied by the arms to one of the rafters in the ceiling of his farm. The body was completely carbonised; but the head, arms, and feet were unburnt. Farther on a child of about 15 was tied up, the hands behind the back, and the body was com- pletely torn open with bayonet wounds. Num- erous torpses of peasants lay on the ground in positions of supplication, their arms lifted, and their hands clasped. The Belgian Consul in Uganda, who is now a volunteer in the Belgian Army, reports that wherever the Germans passed the country has been devastated. The few inhabitants who remain in the villages tell of the atrocities committed by the enemy. Thus, at Wacker- zeal, seven Germans are said to have succes- sively violated a woman and then to have killed her. In the same village they stripped a young boy to the waist, threatened him with death, holding a revolver to his chest, pricked him with lances, and then chased him ,into a field and shot at him, without, however, hitting him. Everywhere there is ruin and devastation. At Buecken many inhabitants were killed, includ- ing the priest, who was over 80 years old. Between Imp& and Wolverthem two wounded Belgian soldiers lay near a house which was on fire. The Germans threw these two unfortunate men into the flames.

OFFICIAL REPORT ON GERMAN ATROCITIES.

Outrages on Bagian Women and Non-Combatants.

The Press Bureau issued on Tuesday afternoon a translation of the second report of the Belgian Commission of Inquiry on the Violation of the Rights of Nations and of the Laws and Customs of War. The report, which was communicated by the Belgian Legation on September 11th, is as follows :

===== 0002601_19140919_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
Antwerp, August 31, 1914. TO MONSIEUR CARTON DE WIART, MINISTER OF JUSTICE.

Sir,-

The Commission of Inquiry have the honour to make the following report on acts of which the town of Louvain, the neighbourhood, and the district of Malines have been the scene:— The German Army entered Louvain on Wed- nesday, August 19, after having burnt down the villages through which it had passed. As soon as they had entered the town of Louvain the Germans requisitioned food and lodging for their troops. They went to all the banks of the town and took possession of the cash in hand. German soldiers burst open the doors of houses which had been abandoned by their inhabitants, pillaged them, and committed other excesses.

The German authorities took as hostages the Mayor of the City, Senator Van der Kelen, the Vice-Rector of ihe Catholic University, and the Senior Priest of the City, besides certain magis- trates and aldermen. All the weapons possessed by the inhabitants, even fencing swords, had already been given up to the municipal author- ities, and placed by them in the Church of Saint Pierre. In a neighbouring village, Corbeck-Loo, on Wednesday, August 19, a young woman, aged 22, whose husband was with the Army, and some of her relations were surprised by a band of German soldiers. The persons who were with her were locked up in a deserted house, while she herself was dragged into another cottage, where she was raped by five soldiers succes- sively..

In the same village, on Thursday, August 20, German soldiers fetched from their house a young girl, about 16 years old, and her parents. They conducted them to a small deserted coun- try house, and while some of them held back the father and mother, others entered the house, and, finding the cellar open, forced the girl to drink. They then brought her on to the lawn in front of the house, and raped her succes- sively. Finally they stabbed her in the breast with their bayonets. When this young girl had been abandoned by them after these abominable deeds, she was brought back to her parents' house, and the following day, in view of the gravity of her condition, she received Extreme Unction from the parish priest, and was taken to the hospital of Louvain, as her life was despaired of.

WOMEN BAYONETTD; MEN BURNED

ALIVE:

_ On August N and 25 Belgian troops made a sortie from the entrenched camp of Antwerp, and attacked the German Army before Malines. The Germans were thrown back on Louvain and Vilvorde. On entering the villages which had been occu- pied by the enemy, the Belgian Army found them devastated. The Germans, as they retired, had pillaged and burned the villages, taking with them the male inhabitants, whom they forced to march in front of them.

Belgian soldiers entering Hofstade, on August 25, found the body of an old woman who had been killed by bayonet thrusts. She still held in her hand the needle with which she was sew- ing when she was killed. A woman and her 15 or 16-year-old son lay on the ground, pierced by bayonets. A man had been hanged. At Sempst, a neighbouring village, were found the bodies of two men, partially carbonised. One of them had his legs cut off at the knees; the other had the arms and legs cut off. A workman. whose burnt body has been seen by several wit- \

===== 0002601_19140919_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LOUVAIN HORROR.

At nightfall on August 26 the German troops, repulsed by our soldiers; entered Louvain panic- stricken. Several witnesses affirm that the Ger- man garrison which occupied Louvain was erroneously informed that the enemy were enter- ing the town. Men of the garrison immediately marched to the station, shooting haphazard the while, and there met the German troops who had been repulsed by the Belgians, the latter having just ceased the pursuit. Everything tends to prove that the German regiments fired on one another. At once the Germans began bombarding the town, pretending that civilians had fired on the troops, a suggestion which is contradicted by all the witnesses, and could scarcely have been possible, because the inhabi- tants of Louvain had had to give up their arms to the municipal authorities several days before. The bombardment lasted till about 10 o'clock at night. The Germans then set fire to the town. Wherever the fire had not spread the German soldiers entered the houses and threw fire- grenades, with which some of them seem to be provided. The greater part of the town of Louvain was thus a prey to the flames, particu- larly the quarters of the upper town, comprising the modern buildings, the ancient Cathedral of , St. Pierre, the University buildings, together with the University library, its manuscripts and collections, and the municipal theatre. The Commission considers it its duty to insist, ' in the midst of all these horrors, on the crime committed against civilisation by the deliberate destruction of an academic library which was one of the treasures of Europe. The corpses of many civilians encumbered the streets and squares. On the road from Tirle- naont to Louvain alone a witness counted more than 50. On the doorsteps of houses could be seen carbonised bodies of inhabitants, who, hid- ing in their cellars, were driven out by the fire, tried to escape, and fell into the flames. The suburbs of Louvain suffered the same fate: We can, affirm that the houses in all the districts between Louvain and Malines, and mist of the suburbs of Louvain itself, have practically been destroyed. On Wednesday morning, August 26, the Ger- mans brought to the Station Squares of Louvain a group of more than 75 persons, including sev- eral prominent citizens of the town, amongst whom were Father Coloboet and another Span- ish priest, and also an American priest. The men were brutally separated from their wives and children, and after having been subjected to the most abominable treatment by the Ger- mans, who several times threatened to shoot them, they were forced to march to the village of Campenhont in front of the German troops. They were shut up in the village church, where they passed the night. About four o'clock the next morning a German officer told them they

had better go to confession, as they would be shot half an hour later,. About half-past four they were liberated. Shortly afterwards they were again arrested by a German brigade, which forced them to march before them in the direc- tion of Malines. In reply, to a question of one of the prisoners, a German officer said they were going to give them a taste of the Belgian quick- firers before Antwerp. They were at last re- leased on the Thursday afternoon at-the gates of Alalines.

It appears from other witnesses that several thousand male inhabitants of Louvain, who had escaped the shooting and the fire, were sent to Germany for a purpose which is still unknown to us.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
BURNT DOWN VILLAGES AND HALF- CRAZY PEASANTS.

The fire at Louvain burnt for several days. An eye-witness who left Louvain on August 30 gave the following description of the town at that time:—

"Leaving Weert St. Georges," he says, only saw burnt-down villages and half-crazy peasants, who, on meeting anyone, held up their hands as a sign of submission. Before every house, even those burnt down, hung a white flag, and the burnt rags of them could be seen among the ruins. "At Weert St. Georges I questioned the in- habitants on the causes of the German reprisals, and they affirmed most positively that no in- habitant had fired a shot, that in any case the arms had been previously collected, but that the Germans had taken vengeance on the popula- tion because a Belgian soldier belonging to the gendarmerie had killed a 'Chian. "The population still remaining in Lonvain have taken refuge in the suburb of Heverle, where they are extremely crowded. They have been cleared out of the town by the troops and the fire. The fire started a little beyond the American College, and the town is ENTIRELY destroyed, except for the Town Hall and the station. Fur- thermore, the fire was still burning to-day, and the Germans, far from taking any steps to stop it, seemed to feed it with straw, an instance of which I observed in the street adjoining the Town Hall. The Cathedral and the theatre are destroyed and have fallen in, as also the library; in short, the town has the appearance of an ancient ruined city, in the midst of which only a few drunken soldiers move about, carrying bittles of wine and liqueurs, while the officers 'themselves, seated in arm-chairs round the tables, drink like their men. "In the streets the swollen bodies of dead horses rot in the sun, and the smell of fire and putrefaction pervades the whole place." The Commission has not yet been able to obtain information about the fate of the Mayor of Louvain and of the other notables who were taken as hostages.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
COMMISSION'S CONCLUSIONS.

The Commission is able to draw the following conclusions from the facts which have so far been brought to its notice :.-- _ .

In this war, the occupation of any place is systematically accompanied and followed, some- times even preceded, by acts of violence towards the civil population, which acts are contrary both to the usages of war and to the most elementary principles of humanity. The German procedure is everywhere the same. They advance along a road, shooting inoffensive passers-by—particularly bicyclists—as well as peasants working in the fields.

_ In the towns or villages where they stop they begin by requisitioning food and drink, which they consume till intoxicated. _ -

Sometimes from the interior of deserted houses they let off their rifles at random, and declare that it was the inhabitants who fired. Then the scenes of fire, murder, and especially pil- lage, begin, accompanied by acts of deliberate cruelty, without respect to sex or age. Even where they pretend to know the actual person guilty of the acts they allege, they do not con- tent themselves with executing him summarily, but they seize the opportunity to decimate the population, pillage the houses, and then set them on fire. '

After a preliminary attack and massacre they shut up the men in the church, and then order the women to return to their houses and to leave their doors open all night. From several places the male population has been sent to Germany, there to be forced, it

Continued in next column.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR PARISH CHURCH.

The Hector Explains its interesting History TO ADULT SCHOOL VISITORS. ,

On Saturday afternoon the Adult Schools had their fifth ramble of the season. On this occa- sion over fifty members, supplemented by a good number of parishioners, were conducted over the Parish Church by the Rector. After giving a hearty welcome to those who had braved the wet, stormy afternoon, Canon Willis gave a brief, lucid synopsis of the chief periods of English architecture before coming to his description of the Parish Church itself. This ant introduction helped to a fuller appreciation of the beauties of the building as they were unfolded later.

HISTORY OF THE BUILDING. - Five churches, the Rector said, have been built upon the present site. The fist was a Saxon church, the second a Norman building, erected about 1150 by Matthceus de Vilars. This church stood for two hundred years, until 1350, the patronage being conveyed about 1250 to Sir William Fitz Almeric de Boteler, the seventh baron, the one who had ,removed his seat from the neighbouring Mote Hill to Bewsey. Of this church some stones are still to be seen in the Municipal Museum. It was taken down about 1350, and in its place was built the third church, in the 'decorated style of architecture by Sir William Fitz William le Boteler, the ninth baron, in 1354, of which the chancel still re- mains. The nave, after standing over four hundred years, was taken down in 1770. his church, judging from a sketch plan preserved in the Museum, seems to have been cruciform, without side-aisles, the two chapels, called the Lady or Boteler, and the Massey, forming the arms. In 1770 the fourth nave was built by Rector Edward Owen, which lasted till 1859. In 1859 and later years, what was practically the fifth church was built; that is to say, all that is now visible, except the chancel and south aisle. It was in 1833 that Rector Powys took down the south wall of the nave and built with the same stones the present aisle.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
'THE BOTELER OR LADY CHAPEL. _ _

The Rector next conducted the -Paity—ii.:l the Boteler Chapel. This deeply interesting por- tion of the church, Canon Willis said, was built by Sir John Fitz William le Boteler, the tenth baron, towards the end of the 14tit century. He was a son of the baron who built the chancel. He died in 1403 and was buried with his wife in a recess on the north side, still marked in the present building by a couple of arches. His great grandson, Sir John Fitz John le Boteler, the 13th baron, is the knight who is believed to have been murdered at Bewsey. He and his wife, Margaret, were buried in 1463. beneath the monument which still stands to his memory in the centre of the chapel. Many other inter- esting details of the great dead who lie buried in the chapel were explained by the Rector.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CHANCEL.

Coming now to the chancel, which is the oldest and most interesting part of the -building, Canon Willis described the internal and external features. From the outside it will be seen that the four topmost courses have been added at a later date, that there is a buttress on the north side, and that there are four windows at the eastern end and sides almost on the level with the ground. The buttress and the windows are the interesting features. They are connected with a crypt, which is a fundamental part of the chancel of 1354. The only access to this chamber originally was by a staircase within the buttress. For 200 years it was probably used by the parish priest both as an oratory and a vestry. This staircase not only descended into the crypt, but ascended towards the roof, but for what purpose is still a mystery. Canon Willis mentioned that probably if the present Lyon tablet on the chancel wall were removed, further particulars might be disclosed, and al- though he had obtained permission to remove this, he had not yet seen his way to do so. So the mystery remains. The stairs may simply have ended in a space for necessaries and valu- ables, or given access to the roof for repairs.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CRYPT.

The crypt was Utie—d. for -260- years, and then for 260 years more, from 1559 to 1824, it was closed. It was probable that in 1559, in Queen Elizabeth's time, the entrance was blocked up, and for 260 years the staircase and crypt was unknown, the earth having accumulated round the windows. It was in the year 1824, during the incumbency of Rector Rawstorne, that its discovery took place, partly by the removal of some panelling which had been placed round the chancel and partly through the piercing of the buttress. Nothing further was done till about 1840, when Rector Powys began altera- tions in the chancel, raising the roof four courses and putting a large modern window into the but- tress, evidently to light the passage into the crypt, which he used as a vestry. Until then there was no access to the crypt, except from the north. In 1841 Mr. Powys made the stair- case on the south side. In order to do this he closed a small entrance into the chancel, and made the steps down into the crypt instead of up into the chancel. The stone pillar with a font-like cup at present at the head, of the crypt staircase in the chancel was formerly the pillar of a sun-dial in the Rectory garden, and was placed in its present position by Rector Queckett.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
COMMUNION PLATE AND REGISTERS.

The party now descended into the crypt where were on view the parish registers, dating from the reign of Queen Elizabeth, the first entry being made on the 23rd of May, 1591, and the valuable .communion plate. The Rector said the plate was all given during a period of less than 160 years, the earliest and most valuable in 1615. This was a covered cup shaped like a loving cup, made in a most beautiful design in silver gilt. It is now used as a chalice on great festivals. Other "pieces" shown were two sil- ver chalice cups of 1627, large silver salver 1694, silver flagon 1699, silver flagon 1720, small silver paten 1722, small silver paten 1731, and large silver gilt flagon 1746...

- - The inclement weather prevented any atten- tion being paid to the exterior of the building, but in closing his delightful description of the church the Rector drew attention to the famous gravestone in the churchyard .of Margaret Robinson, with its eccentric epitaph. It reads: " This maid no elegance of form possessed, No earthly love defill her sacred breast, Hence free she liv'd from the deceiver man, _,_ _ _

Heaven meant it as a blessing, she was plain." Afterwards the party were very generously entertained to afternoon tea in the Rectory by Canon and Mrs. Willis. Before separating, Mr. Harold Gray, of the Bewsey School, and Mr. G. A. Dunlop, of Sankey Bridges School, on behalf of the members, expressed the warm apprecia- tion of the delightful and memorable afternoon spent, under the guidance, instruction and hos- pitality of the Rector and Mrs. Willis, and said that the Adult School members of all denom- inations were proud of the historic building which they had visited that afternoon, and its 1 long line of worthy Rectors, and of the great part they had played in the religion, life and history of the town.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
WORK OF THE MODERN ATTlLA— continued.

appears, to work at the harvest, as in the old days of slavery. There are many cases of the inhabitants being forced to act as guides, and to dig trenches and entrenchments for the Ger- mans. Numerous witnesses assert that during their marches, and even when attacking, the Germans place civilians, men and women, in their front ranks, in order to prevent our sol- diers firing. The evidence of Belgian officers and soldiers shows that German.detachments do not hesitate to display either the white flag or the Red Cross flag, in order to approach our troops with impunity. On the other hand, they fire on our ambulances and maltreat the ambulance men. They maltreat and even kill the wounded. The clergy seem to be particu- larly chosen as subjects for their brutality. Finally, we have in our possession expanding bullets which had been abandoned by the enemy at Werchter, and we possess doctors' certificates showing that wounds must have been inflicted by bullets of this kind. The documents and evidence on which these conclusions rest will be published in due. The President, (Signed) COOREMAN. The Members of the Commission, (Signed) COUNT GOBLET D'ALVIELLA. „ RYCKMANS. CHURCHES AND THE WAR.

OUR CAUSE A RIGHT ONE.

Prayers Asked for Our Brave Fighting Men.

The Wycliffe P.S.A. service was presided over on Sunday by the secretary, Mr. B. Farrar, who specially referred to our brave men who had gone to fight for King and country. Several of their own brethren, he said, had gone to the front, and for these he asked prayer, that sol- diers and sailors alike, they might be afforded divine protection. Let them beseech God that. in His great mercy, this and all war should be speedily concluded. In speaking upon "Churches and interces- sion," Mr. Hamblett said we could truly acknow- ledge God, seeking His guidance and deliver- ance, because our cause was a right one and a just one. Mr.. Healey led the prayer, and Mr. W. H. Payton expressively rendered an organ solo, which was much appreciated.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
IS THE WAR A LESSON FROM GOD? Addresses at Free Church Open-air Service.

The last of the series of out-door meetings arranged by the Warrington Free Church Coun- cil was conducted on Sunday evening in Cor- poration-street, when a large number of people assembled. Mr. John Griffiths presided, and ad- dresses were given by the Revs. H. T. Dixon (the newly-settled Penketh Wesleyan minister) and W. T. Jaye (of Emmanuel Church).

The Rev. 11. T. Dixon, in the course of an earnest address, said he was reminded that night of one of the greatest generals that ever lived, one who was not only a great general but a good man—he who led the Israelites into Canaan. That man, when he realised that his life's work was done, gave his followers a final message: "Now, therefore, fear the Lord, and serve Him in sincerity and in truth; and put away the gods which your fathers served." He (the preacher) thought that was a message which we needed to-day, and he believed God was teaching it by means of the great calamity that had come upon us. We had a fuller revela- tion of God than the Israelites had thousands of years ago; we could not only serve Him, but love Him. But though we had not, it was true, bowed down like heathens to gods of wood and stone, we had worshipped other gods—gods of materialism and wealth. These we must put, aside, serving Him alone. The Rev. W. T. Jaye said that Paul, in the city of Athens, found men 'worshipping an "un- known God," but many men nowadays refused to worship a God whom they could not see. They must, however, have reliance on God in times of trouble. He would remind them that every moment of their lives they were relying on some force—call it Nature or what they would. In the same manner they must learn to rely upon God.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
PRAYER FOR OUR FIGHTERS. Free Churches Hold Intercession Meeting.

Although the weather was bad, there was a good attendance at the meeting for intercession held by the Warrington Free Church Council on Wednesday night, in the Bold-street Wes- leyan Church. The chair was occupied by Mr. J. C. Holland, and the Rev. T. T. James gave an address. There were also on the platform the Revs. William Reid, W. Thompson Derry and J. S. Chedburn.

The Rev. W. Thompson Derry having offered prayer,

- Chairman said they had gathered for the purpose of deep and sincere prayer. During this critical period, not only in our own, but also in the world's history, it was very necessary that we should come together to humble our- se/ves before the Heavenly Father and His Throne of Grace, pleading for that divine guid- ance and help which He alone could give. The Rev. William Reid read the lesson, after which the meeting was thrown open for prayer. The Rev. T. T. James then gave the address. He asked his hearers particularly to remember those who had gone awaysfrom our own town. Many of them were in danger, and had made great sacrifices to go. We were apt to give oui sympathy to those who had wives and families, but we must not forget that the young men also. had made sacrifices—many of them had given up splendid careers and opportunities in order to go and fight for us. This age had, of course, been interested in religion, but that interest had been more from an intellectual than a spiritual standpoint Now, 4ve must all be feeling that we had been drawl back very close to God. We had learned to believe in prayer, not to regard it as an ex- periment. He trusted that we should, in the future, try to serve God more faithfully than we had done in the past. Before the meeting broke up the Chairman said he hoped they would continue their prayers for the preservation of their country after they had left the meeting, which had, he trusted, been an inspiration to them.

An Inventory. A Canadian lawyer tells this story:— A bailiff went out to levy on the contents of a house. The inventory began in the attic and ended in the cellar. When the dining-room was reached, the tally of furniture ran thus: "One dining-room table, oak. One set chairs (six), oak. . "One sideboard, oak. "Two bottles whisky, full." Then the word "full" was struck out and replaced by "empty," and the inventory wtnt on in a hand that straggled and lurched diagonally across the page until it closed with: "One revolving doormat."

On Friday, September 11th, the prices of Sugar in all our Branches were increased to 31d. for Granulated, and 40. for Cube Sugar. The reason for this action was the receipt of .the following notice :

"The Government have deckled that ALL Retailers of Sugar MUST charge Rd. per lb. for equal to Granulated, and 41d. per lb. for Cube AS MINIMUM PRICES. "Should any Retailer decline to sell at these Minimum Prices, SUPPLIES WILL BE STOPPED by an arrangement between the Government and the Refiners."

We felt we had no option than to fall into line as requested, although WE WOULD RATIIER OUR MEMBERS HAD STILL BEEN ALLOWED TIIE BENEFIT OF OUR CONTRACTS AT TIIE OLD PRICES.

We believe that in certain directions Sugar is being offered under these rates WITH OTHER GOODS, but this is being brought before the notice of the Special Commissioners.

As our Members are perfectly aware, our Profits are all returned as Dividend on Purchases, and THEREFORE THESE INCREASED CHARGES EVENTUALLY COME BACK TO THEM, and do not go to swell private profits.

For the Committee of Management, J. JARMAN, Secretary.
