AN IMPRESSIVE PROROGATION.

Yesterday witnessed one of the most impressive prorogations in the history of the British House of Commons. There may have been stirring scenes in the days of the Stuarts, when the King and his people were unhappily at strife; but the experience of yesterday was conspicuous for its display of solidarity, as well as of determination. By a strange coincidence a report that Ger- many had been discussing with America the terms upon which she was prepared to accept peace had made its appearance. The mention of peace by arrangement at the present time is of itself ridiculous, and, although Ger- many has done some stupid things, it is difficult to believe that her Ruler is quite so bereft as to venture upon the making of any overtures or upon stipulating conditions which would leave the world open to another up- heaval in the course of a few years. The burden of this KAISER " feeler " is that the KAISER resents our declaration for a fight to a finish. The recognition that we have so declared is about the only reliable phrase in the whole story. We are going on to the end, and, as Mr. F. E. SMITH bluntly explained matters, peace will be signed in either Berlin or London—Berlin for preference. We have a long account to settle with the KAISER, in which cold-blooded murder, and even Worse cruelties, will form a large part of the indictment. The KING 's Speech gave the answer to the KAISER, as if in anticipation of this re- markable telegram. His MAJESTY thanks the Commons for the liberality with which they have met a great emergency, and speaks of our fruitless efforts in the cause of peace having

_

===== 0002601_19140919_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 19,1914.

been followed by a spontaneous and WARRINGTON HAN AT MONS enthusiastic rally in every part of the Empire to the flag. "We are fighting for a worthy purpose," declares the Gordon Highlander's Stirring Speech--meaning the maintenance of • Experience. treaty obligations, the protection of the public law, and the vital interests of 170 VIEWER THE ROLL CALL OUT OF the Empire—and we shall not lay down 1,000. our arms until that purpose has been fully achieved. That is the standing answer to the Berlin Butcher, to the end of the chapter. But the great scene came at the close. Mr. WILL CROOKS electrified the House by asking if it would be in order to sing "God save the King." In order or out of order, it was in season, and the staid Commons were soon render- ing the National Anthem, we venture to say, with more meaning attached to its words than ever was before the case. When that demonstration ceased, Mr. CROOKS called "God save Ireland," and the Irishmen cheered when Mr. JOHN REDMOND cried "God save Eng- land, too." Surely there never was a time when the KING'S subjects at home and abroad were so bound up in one interest. It is a concentration of effort that will carry us to certain success. The Royal Assent was given to the Home Rule Bill and the Welsh Dis- establishment Bill, with their Suspen- sory Bill. It was unfortunate that the closing days of the Session should have been marred by Mr. BONAR LAW'S out- burst against the honour and integrity of Mr. ASQUITII. The times and Mr. ASQUITH'S reputation make it unneces- sary and inadvisable to reply to Mr. LAW, further than to say that time will show once again that he is no leader, or that, at best, he is a leader to be repudiated.

A graphic story of the fierce and determined fighting at Mons is told by Private Smiley, a Gordon Highlander, whose relatives reside at 183, Knutsford-road, Warrington. In an interview with "The Examiner" repre- sentative, Private Smiley spoke of the cruelties [ and barbarities committed by the German hordes, and of the destruction they have wrought upon hapless villages and towns. On Thursday, August 13th, he relates, the Gordon Highlanders were warmly welcomed by the French, whilst the vast majority of people in England were still unaware that British troops were on Continental soil. "It was four o'clock in the morning of Sun- day, August 23, when we marched out of our billets," said this gallant Highlander. "We were with the eight], infantry brigade, which would be about 5,000 strong. In my battalion there were over a thousand brave soldiers, but when the roll was called after the battle at Cambrai only 170 were present to answer to their names. I am one of five, who are all that can be accounted for from my company. We took up a position on the extreme flank of the British force, with Mons somewhat to half-left on our rear. We cleared the foreground of willows, beans and everything of that nature, which gave head cover, until we had a good clear range of 2,000 yards. Then the trenches were dug, and what labour was put into them! They were ex- cellent trenches, and it was no doubt owing to the care that was bestowed upon them that we owed our escape from even greater casualties."

A FIERCE ARTILLERY DUEL. Pte. Smiley went on to say that at 11.30 the same morning a fierce artillery duel, which lasted for hours, was commenced between the Germans and the R.F.A. The latter were sta- tioned near a wood in the rear of the High- landers, and consequently the big gun fighting raged furiously right over their trenches. After this had continued for hours, German infantry were observed to be manceuvring. This move- ment was evidently intended for the Gordons, for, if the thousands of Germans had succeeded in entering a wood in the vicinity, the British position would have been untenable. However, the German move was not successful. They came along in companies of about 150 men at a time, but the poor devils were hurled into eternity. A terrific, murderous Maxim fire mowed them down. From the trenches it was possible to take deliberate aim at the Germans as they came up under the shelter of their dead and dying comrades. At five o'clock they evi- dently found their task was hopeless, for their infantry retired. "Their artillery fire is wonderful," said Pte. Smiley, "but their infantry fire—well, it is con- temptible; indeed, it is a shame to shoot the beggars. "Relief was sent to the Royal Irish Regiment, who had been terribly cub up. They, together with the Middlesex, had been surprised as they were at dinner. Mess tins were being filled from the dixies,' When the enemy opened fire. Re- inforcements were sent and we advanced along the road, where a ditch and the road camber gave cover. Along the side of the road a white house stood by itself in the clearing, and imme- diately our officer had passed it, the Germans opened a damnable volley on the house. The singular part about it is that I was the only one to be hit. I was caught on the knee with a piece of shrapnel, and had to dig it out with my knife. However, it is quite well again," and in proof of his assertion the gallant private bared his knee—which bore signs of a newly-healed wound—for our interviewer's inspection.

Ist King's Liverpool Regiment, written as lie lay in hospital. The letter states: "I am getting on very well and have gained about a stone since I have been in hospital. I was like a frame when I came in. . . . We have had a very rough time of it. We were five days under fire and I don't think we had three hours' sleep all the time. It was a terrible time. . . . We were getting mowed down like corn. I don't know how I escaped getting wounded before I did, for I had some narrow escapes. . . . It was about 11.30 on August 25th, or 26th, that the battle started, and it was on all the time until it grew dark. We were in a little village, dodging in and out of the houses, with bombs dropping all round us.

Women and Children Shot Down.

"The worst part of it was to see the women and children running here and there, and get- ting shot down like dogs, whilst their homes were blown to atoms. We did not know the enemy were so near. We were surprised more than anything else, but when we got over the surprise we showed them that two sides could play the game. We had been on the march since the Friday morning, and we were expect- ing a rest on the Sunday, but instead we had to spend tie day dodging bullets and shrapnel. The firing ceaSed at dark, and we thought e might be able to get a bit to eat and a few hours' sleep. However, we only bad tune for a bit of biscuit-and a mouthful of tea, when we had to pack up and go and dig our trenches. The officer in charge told us that when we had finished we could have a sleep, so we worked like niggers, thinking that we should be able to snatch a couple of hours' rest, for we could hardly keep our eyes open. "Spotted " by the Searchlight.

"We were going on nicely and were just fin- ishing and putting straw in the bottom of the trench, when out flashes the enemy's search- light. It had us spotted in no time, and, of course, we were done out of our sleep again. We had to leave the trenches about an hour before daylight, and we only got away just in time, for about twenty minutes later they were blown to atoms. We were at it again all Monday and all Monday night, but things eased up a bit on the Tuesday, so we retired on to another v'.llr.ge. There we got some good billets and we settled down to have a sleep; we did not want any- thing to eat. All we wanted was a sleep. Well, things went all right for about half an hour, when, all at once, our motor transports came dashing up the street, together with men on horseback, whilst the women and children were screaming The Germans-are all round us!'

===== 0002601_19140919_art0065.txt | included=true | included =====
PASSING WEEK,

SILVER BULLETS.

Mr. Lloyd George, when he was approached by the Association of Municipal Corporations with a request to make financial arrangements that would benefit local authorities during war

time, received the deputation sympathetically, and made it quite clear that central and local authorities must, in the present circumstances, work together like "partners," and so help to bring the old country through to a triumphant , conclusion. On the one hand local authorities will be expected to provide work should unem- ployment assert itself, and that will mean spending money. On the other hand public works not needed either in themselves or as a means of relieving distress, should be postponed so as to keep the way clear for Governrmmt finances; for, as the Chancellor of the Exche quer declared, it is the financial staying power of the parties to the war that will win? "We have won with the silver bullets before," he said; and we shall again add the finishing touch with those bullets when British couroe and tenacity have played their part.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
LUCKY WARRINGTON.

This week we interviewed Alderman Peacock, the Mayor, and had from him official confirma- tion of our impression that Warrington, by its prosperity, will be one of the places that will be able to supply the Chancellor with his precious silver bullets in two ways. The trade of the town is very good, and "business as usual" is the thing that will keep the finances of the country in a healthy state. In the second place, we shall not, -if present appearances count for anything, have to go into the. market for money to take upon ourselves relief work, and so we shall not entrench upon the reserires that the Government may need. Should need unfortunately arise to alter this fair prospect in any way, Warrington is fully prepared. With the existing shortage of labour the public work of the town will proceed along normal lines. To use his Worship's words, we are not going to "rush forward" the many schemes that had been begun or mapped out before the war began, but we shall go along normally, and, if need be, there is plenty to be done. By the establishment of a sinking fund, for which powers will be sought, the repayment of £25,000 of loans with interest could be lifted out of this year's accounts and carried over to some future time. This would not only make posterity pay for some of the anxieties we are now bearing for them, but would release that respectable sum for immediate use without in- flicting any corresponding burden upon us.

WORK AHEAD. With or without this sum, his Worship says that the various committees are prepared to get to work the moment the need is made apparent. Running rapidly through the work that could be found by the different departments the Mayor informed us that the Highway De- partment could find work for from 40 to 60 men for six months completing Chester-road, and similar work. Warrington Bridge is ap- proaching completibn, and there will be the river wall, the bridge approaches, and Knuts- ford-road to be attended to. Buttermarket- street and the Circus are suggestive at the present moment of future activity. The Water Department could find work for fourteen men for a few weeks, and the Cemetery Committee could provide for a few more. The Victoria Park extension, with its ten-acre lake, the Lovely-lane school and recreation ground, Liverpool-road, Manchester-road, Win- wick-road recreation grounds, and Oakwood- avenue school and recreation ground are all among the possible suppliers of work. There is immediate employment for 30 men to lay' electricity mains and 12 to lay gas mains. This does not by any means exhaust what we could do—for there is always in prospect the re-sevvering of the town at the cost of a quarter of a million. The Mayor does not think the sewerage of the town is as bad as it is repre- sented to be; that, of course, is a matter of opinion; but a Special Committee is tackling that subject, and it will be dealt with as circum- stances permit. 'Whichever way we regard the situation, therefore, Warrington is in a strong position.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
WASTEFUL ECONOMY..

We are glad to note that the excessive caution that was observable at the outbreak of war is gradually giving way to a more rational condi- tion of things. It was necessary to urge the most rigid economy in face of the prospect that lay before us, but there is a vast difference between prudence and the class of economy that some people adopted—economy that would if persisted in have brought industrial ruin in the wake Of war. Allowing for a certain shrinkage of income and wage-earning powers there are a great number of people who can continue to live under practically normal con- ditions, and it is their duty to the nation to.con- tinue to do. so. The needless throwing out of work of servants and workpeople is a criminal kind of economY, and the abstinence from spending on necessaries that one can afford to buy is wasteful rather than economical. We live by working for each other, and those who wilfully endeavour to alter normal conditions represent so many broken cogs that hinder the smooth running of the complicated mechanism of our nation. We must all help. The circulation of money is an absolute necessity to keep the wheel of industrial life moving, and employers and manufacturers cannot do their share if there is little or no demand from the public for many articles of everyday use. Some are at the front doing their part, and doing it well. Those at home can render valuable help to trade by keeping it on the move and by purchasing what they need, without undue anxiety for the mor- row. The homely phrase that has come to the fore, "Business as usual," or as near the usual as it is possible to make it, expresses the situa- tion that all true patriots are endeavouring to maintain.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
STORIES OF OUTRAGES "ONLY TOO TRUE."

" Yes, the stories of the outrages you hear about are only too true," Private Smiley con-

tinued. "For instance, we came across a small boy, probably about 16 years of age. His thigh was shattered. Civilians have been killed and hospitals blown to smithereens. These things I have seen with my own eyes, and am not re- peating from mere hearsay. One old woman who came into our lines had been driven insane by the horror of it all. God knows what she had gone through, and what became of her dur- ing our retreat! Such stories are almost too horrible for newspapers, but I see that a man in the South Lancashires states that they came across an old woman whose body was slashed with bayonet wounds. Perhaps it was the same old soul.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
HELD THE GERMANS FOR A DAY.

"By the time we reached the Royal Irish Regiment's lines dusk had set in, but the sky was reddened by the flames from four burning villages. The Uhlans made a faint-hearted at- tack upon us, which we easily repulsed. At nine o'clock we were ordered to retire—a pitiful handful against the thousands of the enemy. "Yet," Private Smiley continued, with a ring of pride in his tones, "we had held the pick of Kaiserdom's troops at bay for a whole day. The fire slackened at the time you in Eng- land were leaving your churches on that memor- able Sunday evening. "During the night," the Private went on, "the retreat was continued, but at two o'clock the following morning the troops halted for a brief rest. Two hours later they started retir- ing again. At five o'clock the Germans re- sumed the attack, but, after about three hours, their fire slackened, and they left the British in peace.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CORDONS IN THE VAN.

"On the afternoon of the following day the Gordons moved out to attack the German in- fantry, which had got within 800 yards of the British. Our officer told us to give them cold feet, and we did it," said Private Smiley. "After a few rounds the enemy thought it ad- visable to retire! As we were proceeding through a turnip field my ankle got twisted, but the 130 Battery R.F.A. gave me a lift on one of their limber waggons, but I had only been with them about ten minutes when they went into action with me! Luckily for me, however, the en- gagement was very- short, and our journey to Cambrai was continued. Here I joined the Royal Scots Fusiliers, and accompanied them into the slaughter. I saw one Englishman here working a battery by himself, his friends hay- ing been killed or wounded. To picture Cam- brai imagine the worst war scenes possible, and then perhaps you will get something like an idea of what it was like. "On the Friday the great retreat was com- menced, and after marching all night we came across the Gordons. They had been terribly cut up! "We were trapped by the Germans, but in a manner which seemed almost marvellous our brigadier actually took us through the German lines on to Ham, where I was placed in a Red Cross train and eventually got to England.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
AN AIR DUEL.

"On the march to Cambrai we witnessed a thrilling duel in mid-air between a British flying man and a German aviator. Both machines were wonderfully handled, but every time the German attempted to rise higher the Britisher ascended further still, thus keeping on top. The aviators were using revolvers. How- ever, the issue was soon settled by the arrival of two French airmen in their machines. Sud- denly the enemy's aeroplane descended, and the German when examined down below was found to be in a shocking state. " There is no doubt that the Germans ase their airmen to good advantage, for, ,when we are in position, they fly overhead and, dropping smoke bombs, give their troops an accurate in- dication of our whereabouts. "On the Tuesday," concluded Pte. Smiley, "I saw what was left of the South Lancashires. Good heavens, they had been cut up !" A few hours after he had accorded our repre- sentative the interview recounted above Private Smiley left Warrington for Aberdeen to rejoin his regiment. He was in the best of spirits, but after three hours in the granite city he was granted an additional month's furlough and he immediately returned to Warrington.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
"GERMANS ALL ROUND US." Women and Children Shot Down.

WARRINGTON WARRIOR'S STORY OF ATTACK ON A VILLAGE. Mrs. Patten, of 6, Heaton-square, Orford- street, Warrington, has received an interesting letter from her husband, Pte. P. Patten, of the

===== 0002601_19140919_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
A False Alarm.

"Well, I never saw such a scene in all my life before. We had to rush out with fixed bayonets, just as we were. Some were only in their trousers, and some had their bare feet. Some with their eyes hardly yet open dug their bayonets in their mates. But it happened to be a false alarm. It was only two German scouts that had caused the commotion, and they got captured. But it spoilt our night's sleep, for we had to go and do outpost duty. Still, after all, the false alarm was a God-send, for had it not made us alert we should all have been slaughtered, for we were surrounded by the enemy. We did not know the full extent of what we had been doing till the next day, when the general told us that we were left in the il- lage to lead the enemy to think that our troops were stopping there, whilst, as a matter of fat, the main body of the brigade got clear away. It was a narrow escape, but we got out, thailli God!"

===== 0002601_19140919_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON WAR INCIDENTS.

War Catches Continued. "I hear the war is all over." "Is that so?" "Yes; all over Europe !" Guarding the Waterworks. Three more special constables were sworn in at the Warrington County Petty Sessions on Wednesday. It was stated that they were wanted to guard the Warrington Corporation water works at Winwick.

Ironworkers for the Frofit. We learn that during the past few days eight employes of the firm of Messrs. Pearson and Knowles, the well-known local iron founders, have offered themselves for active service. Proposed Town Guard. In addition to several rifle clubs already in existence, others are being formed, and together with a body known as the Preparatory Train-, ing Corps, these will, if necessary, be drawn upon to form a Town Guard. Padgate Lads on Salisbury Plain. Many Padgate lads have joined the Royal Field Artillery, and are training at present on , Salisbury Plain. They enjoy it. Writing home this week, one of them declares it is quite exhilaratinir to rush round the plain, seated upon a field gun.

A Practical Method. At the conclusion of the entertainments at_a certain Warrington picture house, the gentlemen patrons are requested kindly to drop one cigar- ette each in a box at the door. The contents of the box are forwarded to the military authori- ties for distribution amongst the " Tommies " at the front.

" Out of the Mouth of Babes." Overheard at a local picture house the other evening :7

Promising Younister : "Is that a British battleship, dad?" Dad: "No; that one belongs to Germany." Promising Youngster (cheering up after a pause of disappointment) : "Oh, but it will be sunk now, won't it?" From Cell to Barracks. At the Borough Police Court on Monday the Chief Constable asked permission to withdraw a charge of drunkenness and disorderly behaviour preferred against John Kennedy, a private in the South Lancashire Regiment, stationed at Orford Barracks. The case stood adjourned from Saturday, when prisoner said he had en- listed. Inquiries proved that to be the truth, and he was now handed over to the military.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
Special _Constables Guard Water Works.

During the week a number of the employes in the Water Department have been sworn in as special constables for the purpose of guarding the reservoirs, &c., against being tampered with by evil-disposed persons. At night two or three of the special constables do sentry-go with loaded firearms at Winwick, Houghton Green, and Appleton, each of the reservoirs being closely watched from dusk to daylight. Young Footballers in K. of K.ls Army

_ Many o-f those who were expected to partici- pate in this season's Intermediate Rugby League have, we are informed, joined Lord Kitchener's Army, despite their youth—for the age limit fixed by the League is 17. The foregoing fact was reported at the last League meeting, and was noted with approval. Some teams, it was stated, had lost as many as six or seven players, whilst practically every club participating have lost at least one.

Warrington Relief Work. Yesterday "The Examiner" representative was informed that during the week the Soldiers' and Sailors' Wives' and Families' Association, whose local office is at the Parr Hall, had re- lieved 125 Warrington families. During the six weeks this relief has been in operation assist- ance has been afforded in over a thousand local cases, the total sum paid out to them amount- ing tO" about £BOO.

Railwaymen as Constables. At the Borough Police Court on Tuesday, 26 employes of the L. and N.-W• Railway Company were sworn in for duty as special constables. Councillor H. Roberts, the presiding magis- trate, congratulated the men upon having offered themselves for that important duty. There was no doubt, he said, that they would carry out the duties devolving upon them to the best of their ability, and would do as well as other rail- waymen throughout the country had done in supporting the War Office by carrying out their wishes and instructions. He felt that it was incumbent upon him to pay that compliment to the railway companies. Novel Way of Helping War Fun. At a meeting of the Scoutmasters attached to the Warrington Boy Scouts' Association, held at Crosfield's Works on Monday, it was unani- mously decided to make a collection of clean newspapers and periodicals throughout the town. the idea being to re-sell the same and devote the money so raised to the local War Fund and charities in the town. Persons desirous of help- ing in the scheme by giving their old papers should drop a postcard to the Association Secre- tary, Mr. Charles J. Newton, c/o The Athletic Stores, Ltd., Market-place. On receipt of such card arrangements will be made to collect the papers.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. CROSFIELD AND THE{ MUNICIPAL ELECTIONS, TERRITORIALS.

Praise for Warrington Patriotism.

THE TASK THAT CONFRONTS THE COUNTRY.

Mr. Arthur H. Crosfield, who recently visited the neighbourhood in which the Warrington Ter- ritorials are stationed, had an excellent opportu- nity of seeing our local men engaged in the prac- tical work of soldiering. Subsequently Mr. Crosfield wrote to the Bat- talion a letter, in the course of which he said : "It was a fine thing to see our men of War- rington acquiring under such first-rate tuition such useful and invaluable training, while at the same time by the healthy, hardy out-of-door life they get into fine form and fettle for any duties that may be imposed on them in the very near future.

"I am certain that it is a source of deep and permanent satisfaction to every man and woman in Warrington that the old town has acquitted itself well and is playing the worthy part in this, the greatest crisis of national European and, as one may add literally and without ex- aggeration, world-wide history." Mr. Crosfield went on to say that he hoped he should not be considered presumptuous if, after so inspiring a visit, he yielded to the strong temptation to offer a word of the warmest and most cordial congratulations to those who are privileged to play a foremost part in this most honourable, as it is by far the mightiest, Struggle in which Britain has ever been engaged. "These superlatives," continued Mr. Crosfield, , "are no exaggeration. True it is that the wonderful and stirring story of Old England, which we all of us learned long ago at school or at our mother's knee, is full of the great deeds of those who have gone before us. Our island story throughout is, as it were, studded with brilliants like the precious stones in the nation's Crown jewels, which they show us in the Old Tower of London. Crecy and Poitiers, Agin- court and Blenheim, Talavera and Trafalgar, and how many more besides, with 'the crown- ing mercy,' as old Oliver Cromwell would have called it, of Waterloo—these are the records of heroic deeds, the memory of which could only perish with the English language.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
MAGNITUDE OF THE ISSUE.

"And it is also true that throughout the long struggle with Napoleon, lasting for twenty years, we were as much fighting Europe's battle as our own. In the language of the younger Pitt, England then exaved herself by her exer- tions and Europe by her example. It was a gigantic struggle. 'Red battle stamps his foot and nations fee! the shock,' wrote Byron of that war a century since, and echoes of its thunder even now after all these years seem still to reverberate in Europe. And yet, Nthen one thinks of the numbers engaged in the ever-memorable and glorious Tictory .of Waterloo, how,they pale into insignificance com- pared with the millions who are taking part in the titanic struggle which has convulsed Europe to-day!

"lint it is not only, nor yet chiefly, because of the immense numbers arrayed in line of battle, but above all because of the Dumber and magnitude of the issues at stake that we all feel this war to be beyond all comparison the greatest task to which the men of Great Britain, Ireland an the Empire have ever set their hand. There is first of all Belgium, and our pledged word to defend her Treaty rights to be made good and upheld.

WHEN THE TYRANT IS ON HIS KNEES. "Whatever else is done, once our great task is accomplished and the tyrant beaten to his knees—and civilised Europe 4ther will nor can be satisfied with anything less—Belgium will be our first consideration. Although it be impossible wholly to repair the ravages she has suffered or to compensate her people for the horror and agony they have endured at the hands of so brutal and barbarous a foe, still what can be, must, and will be done. "When that time comes selfishness or mean- ness in such a case is unthinkable. Men will vie with each other in showing their apprecia- tion of the immense and incalculable service she has rendered to Europe, and their admiration for her fortitude and the undaunted courage with which she faced the overwhelming forces brought against her. I do not think that ad- miration will ever be better expressed than it was in the Prime Minister's speech in the House of Commons. That short speech—the most elo- quent I venture to think of our times—found an echo in the hearts of men all over the world. and one would not envy any man who could read unmoved the passage in which is paid fhe supreme—and how well deserved—honour of comparison with the greatest heroes of all anti- quity, the men of Athens and Sparta. "Then linked with Belgium is France, whose fair face has been—though one hopes and be- lieves for the time being only—marred and dis- figured by all she is suffering at the hands of a ruthless invader. We are fighting to avenge Prance as well as Belgium, and for a victory which will for all time protect them both from the reckless, overbearing, unscrupulous greed of a coarse and brutal neighbour. And Holland, too, and Switzerland and Denmark. There is indeed no limit to the megalomania with which we have to reckon, with which we have grappled, and which we must, crush. Russia and France may be more immediately concerned, but if the modern Hun conquered them our turn would come next. Therefore, taking the long view of things, we are also fighting for nothing less than the integrity and independence of the British Empire. Finally, above all and including all these great objects., we are fighting to make law and justice prevail and triumph over brute force and the fraud that dogs its footsteps. "Incidentally one might add as a further ob- • ject that it is really about time, and only an • act of common charity, to cure the Kaiser and , the yunkers of Prussia of their insane egotism and that grievous malady known as "swelled , head."

"Now I do not know whether any of our Battalion will have had the courage or the patience to follow me this far; but if they have I think I hear it forcibly suggested that when we are through with the work on hand our reckoning with the Kaiser and the Germans will be of a decidedly sterner character. It will, indeed. lam not going to prolong this letter by indulging at any length in premature speculations as to the future. Our Foreign Secretary spoke words of wisdom when he warned Germany of the incalculable conse- quences of such a war, and it cannot be very long before she begins to realise the, for her, appalling truth of that warning.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
KAISERDOM DOOMED.

"In the meantime some things seem pretty clear. For instance, it is clear that Kaiserdom, I as we have known it in Germany and Austria, will be known no more; nor yet the German and Austrian Empires as we see them to-day. I think, too, we must hope and believe that in- stead of two rival groups in Europe, a system that has been productive of untold mischief, we may have one strong central authority—the powerful nucleus of which we already have in our firmly united Allies—under whose eeg is the smaller nations of Europe would live and work out their destinies in peace. The very exist- ence of such a centralised predominant author- ity would give Europe instant relief and unload from the shoulders of the nations a vast deal of the excessive expenditure of recent years due to that frenzied competition in armaments, for which Germany has been fundamentally respon- sible. These are all objects of the highest im- portance to Europe and to civilisation at large, and what magnificent service our Navy and our gallant Army have rendered in helping to ac- complish them.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0079.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR SPLENDID MEN.

"Which of us can read without feelings of the deepest and most legitimate national pride the splendid tribute to our Army paid by *General French in the report we read in this morning's papers? Theirs is a record which will rank with the greatest and bravest achievements in our history. Therefore once again may I offer very whole-hearted congratulations to every Warring- tonian in camp, privileged, as our fellows are,

Cheshire and _Distress.

In our advertisement columns appears a list of subscriptions in answer to the appeal of the Lord Lieutenant of Cheshire for the National Relief Fund.

Schools for Men and Women. Schools for men and women! That is the latest departure of the Warrington Education Committee. There are three—Evelyn-street, Bolton and Beamont—for the women, and only one for men, and that is in Evelyn-street also. The women's schools commence their evening work on the 29th of this month, and all over 21 years of age may attend for the study of dress- making, cookery and millinery. The fees are low (single subject, 35.; two subjects, 55.), and there will be no examinations. The men's school opens on October Ist, and only under exceptional circumstances will anyone under 21 years of age be admitted. The subjects taught will be "the three R's," and again there will be no examinations. The fee for the session is 3s.

Mrs. Lyon Detained at Baden-Baden. Mrs. Lyon, of Appleton Hall, has been experi- encing some of the inconveniences of the war abroad. She was staying with her daughter, Lady Acton, when hostilities began, but as she had no papers with her she was treated like all other Britishers held up by the war. Lord Acton, Charge d'Affaires at Darmstadt, the capital of the Grand Duchy of Hesse, who was on duty as Lord-in-Waiting to King George, immediately hastened to the Continent and dis- covered that her Ladyship and family, with numbers of other British tourists, had been transported to Baden-Baden. It is stated that it is largely owing to the influence of Lord Acton that all the British at Baden-Baden will be allowed to return home, provided they can satisfy the German authorities as to their bona- fides, and are not available for military service. The latest message received at Appleton Hall is to the effect that Mrs. Lyon and family are safe at Berne.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
War and Evening Classes.

The eight evening schools—for men, youths, women and girls—the Technical and Commercial Institutes, School of Art, Beamont, Bolton, Heathsidel Evelyn-street, and Fairfield—com- menced their work for the winter last week. No definite figures are as yet to hand, but we understand that the attendance of the young women and girls, at the domestic and other classes, has been very satisfactory. There is a decrease in the attendance of youths, which is attributed to the element of excitement at present pervading the town, and to the fact that in many cases fathers of suitable lads and lassies are away at the front. The local Educa- tion Authorities hre doing all they can to remedy this, by means of letters to the parents, impressing upon them the fact that at this time the country is needing a steady and an intelligent manhood more than ever before. Of course, they have no compulsory powers regard- ing attendance at evening schools. . Warrington Teachers' Association. At a well-attended meeting of the Warring- ton and District Teachers' Association, held on Friday of last week, the following resolutions were passed unanimously :—(a) "That .110 be granted from the reserve fund to the National Relief Ftind—ts to be paid to the N.U.T. Special Fund, and to the Mayor's Fund"; (b) " Thal, the Mayor be assured, in the event of any local fund for the relief of distress being initiated, that the teachers of Warrington will do their utmost to help such a fund"; (c) "That mem- bers be urged to offer their services for clerical work in the administration of the fund"; (d) "That an organised effort be made to create a fund to relieve distress caused by the war, the allocation of such a fund to be at the disposal of the association." The amount of the weekly or monthly subscription and the method of col- lection are to be left entirely to the staffs of the various schools; but it is hoped that there will be a liberal response and that each school col- lector will forward contributions at least once a month to the honorary treasurer (Mr. J. Upson, Silver-street School).

Death of Mr. William Jackson. We regret to announce the death of Mr. Wil- liam Jackson, of 249, Liverpool-road, which took place at the Infirmary on Friday night last week after a short and painful illness. The deceased gentleman was born at Chester and was in his 70th year. He came to reside in Warrington 25 years ago. Prior to removing to Warrington he had been in the employ of Messrs. Walker, Parker and Co., of the Chester Lead Works, where he was associated with Mr. W. Sloane, now managing director of the Mersey White .Lead Works, and his father. During the whole of his residence in Warrington the deceased was in the employ of the Mersey White Lead Co. as foreman cooper. On the formation of the Little Sankey Parish Council he was elected a member' of that body, and continued in office until the district was taken over by the Warrington Cor- poration. He took a deep interest in the War- rington Infirmary and had been a member of the Working Men's Committee of that institu- tion for a number of years. He was a most devout Churchman, and worshipped at St. Luke's Church, where he held office as Vicar's warden and sidesman. He belonged to the Independ- ent Order of Oddfellows, M.U., and for 52 years had been a member of the Hearts of Oak. Touching reference to the death of Mr. Jackson and the loss sustained by St. Luke's Church and parish, was made by the Bishop of the Diocese in his address to the churchworkers in Holy Trinity Church on Tuesday evening. The in- terment took place on Wednesday at the War- rington Cemetery, the first portion of the ser- vice being held in St. Luke's Church.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
MUSICAL SOCIETY SEASON. Proposed Performance of "Elijah."

GENERAL OUTLOOK. The members of the Warrington Musical Society assembled in the Wycliffe Memorial Hall on Tuesday evening for the first rehearsal of the opening season under Mr. F. 11. Crossley's conductorship. Despite the prevalence of war's alarms and the fact that, owing to the exigen- cies of present-day conditions, the society has had to find a new meeting-place, there was an excel- lent muster of vocalists and instrumentalists already on the society's membership roll, and of candidates for new membership. The fact that " Elijah" has been selected for rehearsal doubt- less accounts for some of the enthusiasm thus shown in the resumption of the society's prac- tices, it being a notable experience of the man- agement that a sterling work such as Mendels- solin's masterpiece is always potent in bringing members together at the commencement of the season in large numbers. The society's committee is naturally just now feeling some doubt in regard to carrying through the season's programme as decided on , before the war began. Since then notification has been given that the Parr Hall is required for purposes connected with the war, and con- sequently the society's concert cannot be given as intended. What other arrangements can be made it is too soon to say, but it is the intention of the committee that the performarce of "Elijah" shall be given in some other building, probably about the third week in November, and, as far as possible, to carry on the work of the society uninterruptedly through the season. Due announcement will be made later, and it is hoped, seeing the society is carrying on a very disinterested and useful work, that subscribers and the public will, in spite of the war, con- tinue their subscriptions and support, and thus help the society " to carry on as usual."

The Opera Section. The work of the Opilra Section of the Musical Society is, for a time at least, in a state of suspense. It is, of course, an open secret that Planquette's "Rip Van Winkle "—well advanced in rehearsal when the war broke out—was in- tended for performance at the usual date in October, and it is whispered that many pleasant surprises were in store for the opera's numerous patrons, amongst whom considerable anticipa- tory interest in regard to these things had been I aroused. In the present state of public feeling it has, we believe, been decided to postpone for the present this ever-welcome event. A month's close application on the part of those concerned is, however, we are credibly informed, all that is necessary to perfect the society's arrange- ments for putting the opera upon the stage, and we are authorised to say that immediately it is seen that the thing can be given with any hope of its proving a financial success, a new date will be fixed upon for the public performances by arrangement with Mr. 11. L. Warburton, the acting-manager of the Royal Court Theatre.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 19,1914.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
. PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS.

ROMANTIC DRAMA AT THE ROYAL COURT.

An excellent repertoire company, headed by Mr. Alexander Marsh, is in possession of the boards at the Royal Court Theatre this week, and they have been, and are, staging plays cal- culated to appeal to every Warrington theatre- goer with a taste for that which is among the best in modern romantic drama. On Monday afternoon they presented an intensely interest- ing stage version of Dickens's immortal novel, "Oliver Twist," whilst in the evening they delighted a large audience with Tustin Huntley McCarthy's "If I Were King," a play dealing, along more or less historical lines, with an episode in the career of the vagrom French poet, Francois Villon, whose graceful jingles and classically-turned rondeaux still illumine the pages of the lyric literature of our Gallic neigh- bours and present gallant allies. Of course; many of the incidents that go to make up the extremely interesting play are purely apocryphal, but the central figure, Master Fran- cois Villon, Master of Arts of the University of Paris, poetaster and roysterer, fashioner of fanciful ballads and swashbuckler, was a very real person indeed in the days of the eleventh Louis of France. The play received excellent treatment at the hands of a most capable com- pany, Mr. Marsh, himself, undertaking thie difficult role of the vagabond ballad-monger with distinct success, whilst Miss Carrie Baillie played up to him splendidly as Katherine de Vancelles. Mr. Wilson Gunning was most effective as the King, and all the other numer- ous parts were carefully taken, the perform- ance as a whole being a polished and entirely satisfactory one, whilst the mounting and cos- tuming left nothing to be desired. For to-night lovers of drama are promised a special treat in the shape of "Nell Gwynne of Drury-lane Theatre," a specially interesting and exciting version of the story of the love of a king for an orange girl, who subsequently be- came the uncrowned Queen of England.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
For Next Week.

"For Auld Lang Syne," ar4 exceptionally powerful drama, will be presented next week by Mr. Bulmer's strong company.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
CLEVER COMEDY AT THE HIPPODROME.

"The Dear Departed," a clever one-act trifle by the late Stanley Houghton, who in the few brief years vouchsafed to him contrived to win a foremost place among contemporary drama- tists, is the outstanding item in an excellent programme at the Hippodrome this week. The playlet is worthy of the brilliant author of "Hindle Wakes," and abounds in rich, whole- some fun, the charm of which is increased for local audiences from the fact that the dialogue IS in the Lancashire dialect. Characterisation and mannerisms are perfect, and the little comedy proves to be one of the most popular and attractive items in an excellent bill. It receives faultless treatment by a company sent "on the road" by Mr. Ernest Dottridge, the well-known North-country impressario, whose name in connection with any theatrical venture is proof of its excellence. Every member of the little company makes the most of the opportu- nities afforded, bringing out the many salient points in a most praiseworthy fashion. Miss Ethel Hardacre and Miss Betty Noel are excel- lent as the jealous, suspicious and critical sis- ters; and Mr. Reg. E. Kendrick plays the part of Henry Slater in a most realistic manner, whilst the same may be said of Mr. David Curtis as Ben Jordan. Miss Florrie Booth makes a mischievous Victoria Slater, whilst Mr. Wilton Marsh pleases in the role of Abel Merryweather, the genial "grandfather," around whose supposed demise the comedy revolves. A marvellously clever juggling turn is provided by Francis, whilst his facetious assistant, Alfred, supplies some original comedy element, which helps the turn along. The Brothers Redmond cause much laughter as acrobatic comedians, their gags and witticisms being smart and up- to-date. Barlow and Brookes score a decided success as a comedy baritone and tenor duo, and Minnie Paget proves herself an able manipu- lator of the concertina. Mollie Baldwin is a charming operatic soprano and renders popular numbers in pleasing fashion. The war pictures, which are up-to-date, are must enthusiastically received. ' Next Week.

Next week's programme includes: Jennie Johns, comedienne; the Krakes; A. E. Burtie; the "Gaumont Graphic"; Chas. Emerald and Maude Dupre; the Mendelssohn Trio; Five Yorkshire Lassies; and Dan Whitley, "a funny little fellow."

===== 0002601_19140919_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
STAR KINEMA.

The splendid programme this week commences with a series of beautiful Swiss winter scenes. "Memories that haunt" tells how a novelist becomes separated from his pleasure-loving wife, but is restored to her after many thrilling ad- ventures, including the wreck of a great liner. "An Undesirable Suitor" goes through many amusing ordeals before winning "father's con- sent." "Stanton's Last Fling" tells how the hero, defrauded in business, absconds with the remaining funds for a final fling on the Con- tinent, but there rescues a beautiful heiress from the toils of an unscrupulous chevalier, and finally marries her. The "Gaumont Graphic" shows many interesting war incidents, and "A Stage Door Flirtation" concludes an enjoyable entertainment.

The second programme included " A Tragedy in the Clouds" and "Lawyer Quince" the latter a filmed version of one of W. W. Jacobs' in- imitable stories.

Next Week.

The two chief pictures -n-e;t-Week will be "The Beggar Princess" • (first three days) and "Ger- mans entering Brussels" (second three days).

===== 0002601_19140919_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
ANDREWS' PICTURE HOUSE.

A sure draw has been booked for next week in the shape of the big production, " Called to the Front," which pictures in vividly realistic fashion all the horrors of a great war, such as that raging at present. The film will be shown thrice daily—at 2.30, 7 and 9—and there will be a special performance in aid of the National Relief Fund on Sunday night at 8.15, when, in addition to the picture some of the members of Crosfield's Band will contribute appropriate music.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
PICTURE HOUSE.

Patrons of the Picture House can always rely on good value for their money, for the pictures that are screened week by week can quite jus- tifiably be termed "The cream of the picture world." "The Mysterious Leopard Lady," and "A Nation's Peril" form the principal attrac- tions for next week, and many other films of a humorous and tragic nature will be shown.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
PICTUREDROME.

The attractions at this cosy picture theatre are as numerous and varied as ever, and we are promised another excellent entertainment next week, when such excellent films as "The Opal Curse" and "The Battle of the Sexes" are to form two of many strong attractions.

Lord Kitchener's Satisfaction With Progress

Made.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
THE TERRITORIALS' PART.

Lord Kitchener, in the House of Lords on Thursday, praised the personal skill and energy of Sir John French. The tide, he said, had turned. Great Britain had in the field sa divisions and two cavalry divisions. More cavalry were being prepared and other units organised from overseas garrisons. The call for recruits in the British Isles has produced "a most remarkable demonstration of the energy and patriotism of the young men of this country." The recruits—"splendid material"—are to be organised into .four new armies. Meanwhile, from the Special Reserve and the Territorial Force the nece,ssaly reinforcements for the Army in the field will be forthcoming.

the information that the train was about to move off.

Hardly had a rousing "good-bye" been said to the first contingent before the sound of dis- tant cheering heralded the approach of a second batch, which proved to be of equal strength to the first, and was bound for the same journey on the same high errand. The crowd ran to meet this further lot of brave fellows, who reached the station by the same route as did their comrades, and who, like them, came along marching and whistling and in the highest feather. If anything they came in for a heartier and noisier farewell than did the first contin- gent, for larger numbers of the people had by now got wind of what was afoot. They made their way into the station between two solid wedges of cheering men and boys, who waved their hats wildly in the air as a kind of accom- paniment to their enthusiastic vocal expression of approval and pride, whilst on the fringe of the surging knots stood many womenfolk who waved handkerchiefs and added their shrill- voiced expressions of good-bye and good luck ta the general chorus.

PARTING HAND-SHAKES. The gallant Tommies received the bravos of the crowd with counter-cheers, and as they filed through the station doors they grabbed at and I heartily shook as many as possible of the hun- dreds of hands held out to them. "Good-bye, 'ma!" said one burly warrior, as he accepted a proferred package from an even more burly lady, who had forced her way into the front rank of the jubilant onlookers, "good-bye, and if you want to send me another present address it to me at Berlin! That'll find me!" The sally was received with laughter and more cheers, amid which the tail-end of the long line of potential heroes disappeared through the portals and clattered down the steps to the waiting train. A few privileged persons were allowed on the station to say last farewells, and the echo of the cheers they raised, as with a slamming of carriage doors, a grunt and a shriek from the engine and the grinding of many wheels on the bright steel rails, the train moved majestically out of the station, reached the dis- persing crowd outside the station and was re- echoed by them as they wended their way home- wards. Yes, it is an inspiring business, this saying of "good-bye" and " God-speed " to gal- lant sons of the old country who go to maintain her traditions on the battlefield, even if the in- spiration be cloaked in the guise of hoarse cheering and the singing of tawdry songs. By the way, one noticed that the song that one would naturally expect to hear on such occa- sions was conspicuous by its absence; just as it was last week when the Terriers went away. We refer to the grand old national song, "God save the King," which binds loyal British hearts together in every land and every clime. Most of the men who made up the two detach- ments that left on Monday night were strangers to Warrington, some of them even coming from as far south as Cardiff and Swansea—hefty hewers of the black coal in the piping days of peace, but now burning with the spirit of patriotism that in all times when peril has threatened this old kingdom of ours has blended all four races—English, Irish, Scotch and Welsh—that go to make it up into one harmo- nious whole—one nation with one ideal, one Imperial people whose unity of heart is so strong that political differences can never divide us in the hour of danger. Come the three corners of the world in arms, And we shall shock them: nought shall max.' us rue,

If England to itself do rest but true. It only remains to add that the men made a good journey to Seaford, near Whitehaven, in Sussex, where they will undergo a little further training before embarking for the French coast and the field where glory awaits." They were under the command of Majors Green and Appleton and Captain Eversley, officers of the South Lancashire Regiment, and Capt. Everett, of the Royal North Devon Hussars.

TEACHERS FOR THE FRONT. Messages from Volunteers from

Beamont School.

The five male teachers from the Beamont Council Schools, Warrington, are now in train- ing for the Army Ordnance Corps. Mr. Mason (the headmaster) has received the following letter from one of the party : A.O.C. (N.S.), Talavera Barracks, Aldershot, September 10th.

- Dear Mr. Mason,—Perhaps you've been ex- pecting to hear from us ere now. We had a restless, otherwise a bleasant journey to Lon- don, arriving there about four o'clock on Tues- day morning, five rather weary and worn soldiers. London before breakfast had very few charms for us. We managed a splendid breakfast at one of Slater's places. We entrained at Waterloo at 8.55 for Aldershot, arriving here at 10.30. We were fortunate enough to find the training depot of the A.O.C. without much trouble. I am not going to grumble over any- thing. What a lot of soldiers! Soldiers every- where. I suppose you've been to Aldershot. But I should fancy Aldershot in peace time very different from Aldershot in time of war, with all the men on active service. Old soldiers in barracks reckon there are 100,000 soldiers and recruits here at present. So far we have been kept together. We sleep in the same tent (the barracks are eight or ten times overtaxed) and drill in the same squad.

Good News. I have good news for you. Furness has been made a drill instructor. This is where Terri- torial training has become of use. It has been pointed out to us that we stand a very good chance of quick promotion here, but there are so many of us for these. The clerking business is altogether overcrowded; still we have as good a chance as many here, and a good start over the majority. Should we be successful in get- ting a clerkship some of us will be sent to France for certain. People who have a knowledge of French will be sent to the base of operations.

Give our kind regards to all. We are in splendid spirits. D. MORGAN JAMES.

A postcard, reading as follows, has also been received by the Misses Hosier and Brock at Beamont School:— _ _

===== 0002601_19140919_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
" M " House, Red Barracks, Woolwich, Saturday.

What a change from Aldershot! We came here last night, arriving in barracks about 11.30 p.m. What a palatial pile this is! Messrs. Beach and Furness have been kept behind at Aldershot—they will both get stripes. The housing at Aldershot was wretched and the food only fair—here it is grand. We are very happy and looking forward to strenuous training. Our corps is made up of very fine fellows on the whole. We have made several friends. I can assure you that the smokes are being thoroughly appreciated. Shall write you a letter when I have more time.

D. M. J.

ANOTHER THOUSAND MEN. Two Detachments Leave Warrington. STIRRING SCENES AT BANK QUAY.

In the highest of spirits and giving voice to the popular songs of the day as they marched through our streets on their way to the railway station, another thousand men left Warrington on Tuesday night to play their parts as soldiers of the Ring. They constituted the new bat- talion which has been recently formed at the Barracks, and which is to be known as the Bth (Service) Battalion of the South Lancashire! Regiment. They were given an enthusiastic send-off, being followed through the streets by admiring and cheering crowds, who accompanied them as far as the entrance to Bank Quay Station, and there reluctantly left them with final vociferous expressions of appreciation of their pluck in offering their lives and limbs— their all—in the service of their country in this the hour of her sore need. It was an inspirit- ing sight as the men swung past with resolution in their steady tramp and a laugh and a song on their lips, and, whilst it brought a sense of pride in one's race to the heart, it also brought some- thing akin to a choke in the throat when one thought, momentarily, of' the perils that they were going so light-heartedly to face. There are few sights so inspiring as that of a body of men who, from sheer pride of race and love of motherland, are prepared, with jest and joke , on their lips, to, if need be, immolate themselves upon the altar of patriotism, satisfied to endure untold risk and awful hardship in return for the proud feeling that they have at least done all they could to uphold the honour, glory and integrity of the country which gave them birth, and of which we, with them, are one and all so justly proud. The real spirit of a conquering race echoed in the steady tramp, tramp of the marching men, and permeated the raucous, shouting and singing, seeming somehow to in- vest even the latest banality of the music hall with something akin to a touch of dignity.

THE MARCH OF THE THOUSAND MEN.

The thousand men departed in two batches, each 500 strong. The first contingent left the Barrack-square at about nine o'clock, and made for the station by the shortest route, viz., by Orford-lane, Tanners-lane, Froghall-lane and Crosfield-street. Very few people were aware of the impending departure of another batch of England's brave defenders, and consequently the crowd waiting round the Barrack gates was a very small one. But the news soon travelled round, and by the time they reached Bank Quay Station they were attended by thousands, ' anxious to give them a parting cheer and word of encouragement. Cheers were exchanged as the men entered the portals of the railway sta- tion, and were kept up incessantly until the warning shriek of the engine's whistle conveyed

OUR SHARE IN THE ROUT.

SEVEN DAYS OF SPLENDID OFFEN- SIVE WORK. British Airmen Supreme.

Issued by the British Press Bureau, Monday, 1.15 p.m. The following report is compiled from infor- mation sent from the headquarters of Field Marshal Sir John French, under date September 11th

A summary of the operations of the British Army in France was issued by the War Office on the 6th inst., and an account, stated to be incomplete, of further operations was issued from the Bureau on Saturday night, the 12th inst. To-day it is possible to give a more com- plete report of the movements of the British force and of the French armies in immediate touch with it. This account is compiled from data received from the front, and carries the operation from September 4th to September 10th, both days inclusive. It will be remembered that the general posi- tion of our troops on Sunday, September 6th, was stated to be south of the Marne, with French forces in line on our right and left. Practically there had been no change in the' situation since Thursday, the 3rd, which marked the end of our Army's long retirement from the Belgian frontier through Northern France. On Friday, the 4th, it became apparent that there was an alteration in the direction of ad- vance of almost the whole of the First German Army. That army, since the battle near Mons on August 3rd, had been playing its part in the colossal strategic endeavour to create a- Sedan for the Allies by outflanking and enveloping the left of their whole line, so as to encircle and I drive both British and French to the south. I There was now a change in its objective, and it was observed that the German forces opposite the British were beginning to move in a south- easterly direction, instead of continuing south- west on to the capital. Our Force " Ignored." - -

Leaving a strong reargurd along the line of the River Ourcq (which flows south and joins the Marne at Lizy-sur-Ourcq) to keep of the French Sixth Army, which by then had been formed and was to the north-west of Paris, they were evidently executing what amounted to a flank march diagonally across our front. Pre- pared to ignore the British as being driven out of the fight, they were initiating an effort to attack the left flank of the French main army, which stretched in a long curved line from our right towards the east, and so carry out against it alone the envelopment which had so far failed against the combined forces of the Allies, On Saturday, the sth, this movement on the part of the Germans was continued, and large advance parties crossed the Marne southwards at Trilport, Sammeroy, La Ferte-sous-Touarre, and Chateau Thierry. There was considerable fighting with the French Fifth Army on the Fiench left, which fell back from its position south of the Marne towards the Seine.

On Sunday large -hostile forces crossed the Marne, and Rushed on through Coulommiers past the British right. Further east they were attacked at night by the French Fifth Army, which captured three villages at the point of the bayonet. A General Advance.

On Monday, the 7th, there was a general advance on the part of the Allies in this quarter of the field. Our forces, which had by now been reinforced, pushed on in a north-easterly direc- tion, in co-operation with an advance of the French Fifth Army to the north and of the French Sixth Army eastwards, against the Ger- man rearguard along the Ourcq. Possibly weakened by the detachment of troops to the eastern theatre of operations, and realising that the action of the French Sixth Army against the line of the Ourcq and the ad- vance of the British placed their own flanking movement in considerable danger of being taken in the rear and on its right flank, the Germans on this day commenced to retire towards the north-east. _

This was the first time that these troops had turned back since their attack at Mons a fort- night before, and from reports received the order to retreat when so close to Paris was a bitter disappointment. Froth letters found on the dead there is no doubt that there was a general im- pression amongst the enemy's troops that they were about to enter Paris. Severe Punishment Inflicted.

On Tuesday, the Bth, the German movement north-eastwards was continued, their rearguards on the south of the Marne being pressed back to the river by our troops and by the French on our right, the latter capturing three villages after a hand-to-hand fight and the infliction of severe loss on the enemy. The fighting along the 'Ourcq continued on this day, and was of the most sanguinary char- acter, for the Germans had massed a great force of artillery along this line. Very few of their infantry were seen by the French. The French Fifth Army also made a fierce attack on the Germans in Montmirail, regaining that place. On Wednesday, the 9th, the battle between the French Sixth Army and what was now the German flank guard along the Ourcq continued. The British corps, overcoming some resistance on the River Petit Morin, crossed the Marne in pursuit of the Germans, who were now hastily retreating northwards. One of our corps was delayed by an obstinate defence made by a strong rearguard with machine guns at La Ferte- sous-Jouarre, where the bridge had been de- stroyed. Pressure Maintained. _

On Thurscia..y7iii; .1-0-th,--ibe—French Sixth Army continued its pressure on the west, while the Fifth Army, by forced marches, reached the line Chateau Thierry-Dormans on the Maine. Our troops also continued the pursuit on the north of the latter river, and after a consider- able amount of fighting captured some 1,500 prisoners, four guns, six machine guns, and 50 transport waggons. Many of the enemy were killed and wounded, and the numerous thick woods which dotted the country north of the Marne are filled with Ger- man stragglers. Most of them appear to have been without food for at least two days. Indeed, in this area of operations the Germans seem to be demoralised and inclined to surrender in small parties, and the general situation appears to be most favourable to the Allies. Much brutal and senseless damage has been done in the villages occupied by the enemy. Property has been wantonly destroyed, pictures in the chateaux have been ripped up, and houses generally pillaged. It is stated on unimpeach- able authority also that the inhabitants have been much ill-treated.

" Interesting Incidents." Interesting incidents have occurred during the fighting. On the 10th part of our Second Army Corps advancing north found itself marching parallel with another infantry force at some little distance away. At first it was thought that this was another British unit. After some time, however, it was discovered that it was a body of Germans retreating. Measures were promptly taken to head off the enemy, who were surrounded and trapped in a sunken road. Over 400 men surrendered.

On the 10th a small party of French under a non-commissioned officer was cut off and sur- rounded. After a desperate resistance it was decided to go on fighting to the end. Finally, the non-commissioned officer and one man only were left, both being wounded. The Germans came up and shouted to them to lay down their arms. The German commander, however, signed to them to keep their arms, and then asked for permission to shake hands with the wounded non-commissioned officer, who was carried off on his stretcher with his rifle by his side. The arrival of the reinforcements and the ' continued advance have delighted the trooris, who are full of zeal and anxious to press on.

French Commander Praises Our Flying Men. Quite one of the features of the campaign on our side has been the success attained by the Royal Flying Corps. In regard to the collection of information it is impossible either to award too much praise to our aviators for the way they have carried out their duties or to over-estimate the value of the intelligence collected, more especially during the recent advance. In due course certain examples of what has been effected may be specified and the far-reaching nature of the results fully explained, but that time has not yet arrived. That the services of our flying corps, which has really been on trial, are fully appreciated by our Allies is shown by the following message from the Commander-in-Chief of the French armies, received on the night of September 9th by Field Marshal Sir John French "Please express most particularly to Marshal French my thanks for services

5

rendered on every day by the English Flying Corps. The precision, exactitude, and regularity of the news brought in by its members are evidence of their perfect organ- isation and also of the perfect tralning of pilots and observers."

To give a rough idea of the amount of work carried out it is sufficient to mention that dur- ing a period of 20 days up to September 10th a daily average of more than nine reconnaissance flights of over 100 miles each has been main- tained.

Our Ascendancy in the Air. The constant object of our aviators has been to effect the accurate location of the enemy's forces, and incidentally—since the operations cover so large an area—of our own units. Never- theless the tactics adopted for dealing with hostile aircraft are to attack them instantly with one or more British machines. This has been so far successful that in five cases German pilots- or observers have been shot in the air and their machines brought to the ground. As a conse- quence the British Flying Corps has succeeded in establishing an individual ascendancy which is as serviceable to us as it is damaging to the enemy. How far it is due to this cause it is not pos- sible at present to ascertain definitely, but the fact remains that the enemy have recently be.. come much less enterprising in their flights. Something in the direction of the mastery' of the air has already been gained. In pursuance of the principle that the main object of military aviators is the collection of information, bomb-dropping has not been in- dulged in to any great extent. On one occasion a petrol bomb was successfully exploded in a German bivouac at night, while from a diary found on a dead German cavalry officer it has been discovered that a high explosive bomb thrown at a cavalry column from one of our aeroplanes struck an ammunition waggon. The resulting explosion killed 15 of the enemy. Some million and a half Germans were being driven northwards and eastwards into the angle of the Meuse and the Sambre, where the broken country does not conduce to the orderly move- ment of immense armies in rapid retirement. Beyond the Meuse the Ardennes present still greater difficulties; while beyond the Sambre lies the ravaged plain of Belgium, no safe place for defeated Germans.

THE GERMAN STAND. The German armies entrenched themselveSlo4 defence. On their right wing their resistance weakened slightly at some roints. Elsewhere their counter-attacks—attempted by day and by night—were repulsed. A despatch from the British headquarters, describing the recent fighting in France from the battle on the Marne until the Aisne engage- ments still proceeding, says the earlier actions between the British and German troops are de- scribed as rearguard affairs, fought by the enemy to shield his retreat. Nevertheless, the results were considerable. Along the Aisne the resistance has been stouter. The British forced several passages over the river on Sunday, but the fighting was continued on Monday with un- abated vigour. In the despatch, which is dated Monday, the 14th, it is said the Aisne engage- ments "may be merely of a rearguard nature on a large scale, or may be the commencement of a battle of a more serious nature."

FOR SOLDIERS AND REFUGEES. A Plentiful Supply of Garments and Comforts.

Mrs. Edelsten reports that the number of articles of clothing received at the Town Hall up to Thursday, September 17th, reached the magnificent total of 1,405 garments, not count- ing a large quantity of miscellaneous second- hand clothing sent for the use of the Belgians. The Warrington Women's Liberal Association sent a splendid donation of 245 garments, and a large parcel also came from the Conservative Club. The clothing was despatched in three bundles—one to the Base Hospital, another to the Belgian Relief Clothing Department, in London, and a third to the Bureau of the Lady Mayoress of Liverpool for the use of the local Territorials, who have suffered severely under canvas in the recent inclement weather. The committee hope to send a parcel to the Belgian Relief Clothing Department each week.
