JOTANGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

Lord and Cottager. Last week's paragraph headed "A Good- natured King," reminded me of another re- markable incident in the like connection. Lord Savile owns considerable estates in the West Riding of Yorkshire, including extensive moors on the East Lancashire border, where each August he goes for the grouse shooting. Once when I lived in that district, while thus en- gaged he spoke highly of the excellent bread with which his table was supplied, and, learning from his steward that this had been baked in a small cottage in the neighbouring village of lleptonstall, directed that thenceforward a quantity should be forwarded to Rufford Abbey week by week. It is therefore by no means un- likely that on the occasion referred to in these aolumns a week ago the bread eaten by King Edward was made in a humble Yorkshire home in a distant hill-place similar in all respects to the more famous scene of the Bronte sisters' triumphs, and, indeed, only a few miles from Haworth.

A Vile Conundrum. "Punch's " worst joke of the war, reproduced in a: corner of last week's issue of this paper, brought to my mind a fifty-year-old case of the kind. In a town in which I lived just half a century ago, one Myers had a big circus. A favourite member of the troupe was a juvenile prodigy known as "Little Bell." The pro- prietor offered a handsome prize for the worst conundrum having this youngster for the sub- ject, and the following by a local tradesman was declared the winner: "What is the differ- ence between the Parish Church clock and Little Bell?—One rings the chimes and the other charms the ring !" Moreover, the victorious one proudly displayed the prize in the window of his shop for some time after the award. Indiscriminate Almsgiving. Let me hint at just one other 'way in which the general public can really help. I remember that when the plan for co-operative action in dealing with vagrancy ,was being discussed by Union representatives intending to combine for the purpose, it was pointed out that the part of the charitable public in the scheme would be to refuse aid to beggars, no matter how distressing their tale of woe, and recommend them to the nearest food station. It is the persistence and wiliness of the tramp himself that is calculated to be the greatest obstacle to the reform, for he is not likely to give up an easy method of ob- taining a living without a fight. So we were told at the time, with much more to the like effect which might now be recalled with profit. "Not more than one in a hundred is a genuine case needing assistance," stated a prominent Poor Law officer, "and if the public will only refuse all and sundry in every way,"and direct them to the nearest point for the midday meal or to the nearest police office, then this roguery will be soon stamped out." "Weary Willie" in Khaki ! While I am far from supposing that every tramp is a scamp, I like not the prop-dial of the Runcorn Guardians that able-bodied young men of the professional roadster class should be com- pelled to 'enlist for service at the front: The intention is undeniably good, but it appears to me to be based on misapprehension—just as in the case of magistrates up and down the country who are suggesting the enlistment of men brought before them for larceny. What does our splendid army want with slackers and was- trels and criminals? Though of course it has not entered into the hearts of these mistaken justices and public representatives to conceive of such a thing, to bring pressure to bear on tramps and bad characters to offer themselves as army recruits might pardonably be resented as a gross insult by the hosts of fine and highly respectable young fellows who are so cheerfully leaving good situations to join in fighting our battles in this unprecedented crisis in the his- tory of the British Empire. Those who seem to imagine that any sort of rubbish may be shot into the ranks of the army at the present junc- ture are at least 100 years behind the age, and need a sharp jerk to bring their minds up to date.

Not Too Old at Forty. The relieving officer for the Budworth distric has forwarded his resignation to the Runcorn Board of Guardians on the completion of 40 years' continuous service under that body. In one union in which I formerly resided for some time there were some half-dozen public servants with a similar record. Among them were the medical officer of health, the clerk to the educa- tion authority, the master and the matron of , the infectious diseases hospital, and a registrar of births, deaths and marriages who had filled more important offices for the greater part of the period in question. One seldom hears it sug- gested that the holders of such positions are too old at 40—to say nothing of bishops and inferior clergy, generals, judges, and eminent politicians. —Food Question. Not very long ago I read somewhere that a distinguished painter employed a small boy from a neighbouring slum as a model. He gave the child some tea, and asked him if he would like brown bread or white, expecting, as is usual with such youngsters, that he would ask for white. The boy, however, asked for brown bread. " Hello !" exclaimed the painter, aston- ished, "do you like brown bread?" "Yes," replied the boy, "it's got more nitrogen in it." Well, at any rate he- was on the right track. I remember how, writing on the general subject about that same period, a friend of mine sub- mitted in the course of his argument that, though we cannot adopt in this country every- thing we see working advantageously in other lands, we cannot deny the fact that the ques- tion of domestic economy is little understood in our midst, and the practice of it little aimed at, till the housekeeper, by years of experience in losses and failures, has arrived at wisdom and discretion. A preliminary training of our girls, as they grow from childhood to womanhood, in the simple arts of management of the home, could certainly do no harm, and would be pro- ductive of more practical good than much that is now taught them, and especially in the appre- ciation they would acquire of the cheaper but equally nourishing foods. Thus my old jour- nalistic acquaintance, and it is pleasurable to be able to add that Warrington at all events has done and is bent upon continuing really good work in that direction. To be sure, there is still plenty of scope for it, and the necessity for such serious effort was never greater than at present. " Values." While the authorities, from Dr. Oldfield on- ward, are discussing in the correspondence col- umns of "The Examiner" this undoubtedly very important matter of food values, the voice of the crank is still heard in the land and the faddist continues industriously to grind his own particular axe. By-the-bye, I remember once reading in "Good Housekeeping" a whole- hearted commendation of the raw-fonder and all his works. The head-line was calculated to- startle the uninstructed reader with a mental Picture of a savage sort of person gnawing a shinbone upon which the smell of fire had not Come; and to that extent grave injustice was for the moment done to the cause advocated. AS the writer explained all in good time, the Tr. _ fooder dotes on raw vegetables, and one of n favourite ways of preparing them is in the form of salads. A favourite dessert with the raw fooder is made of uncooked pot cheese with quantity of seeded, chopped raisins a gebealaternincto t, a generous amount of orange and lemon juire d mice, a very -little of grated orange peel. Th We are assured, is rich and nutritious, and of aneovel and delicious flavour, so there! To add just Onete softmore sexampleidepart oo ff many, a dish is made from h figs, grated raw onion, olive pars fig,oilt, and grated on o nuts, in the proportion of five partßrazil i onion, two parts olive oil, one Part grated or proteid nuts, and one part ingredients are carefully blendelemea juice. Thd, and the taste of the onion is not detected. The flavour of the dish is delicious, it is again declared. Of all that I read in the comprehensive article from which I now quote, this is the sum : The ln amount that a raw-fooder eats at a meal is small lie is quickly filled. The reason is that the stomach seems to know when it has had enough of the elemental things from which it draws its strength, and telegraphs the fact to the fasts, and the eater finds himself satisfied. On the other hand, with cooked food he goes on eating and eating, and the stomach delays its message, as it has to receive a vast bulk of stuff, largely waste matter, because of the life being cooked out of the food by fire. Are we then to abandon those cookery classes as superfluous and even harmful? I trow not. Still, it is interesting to know of the existence of these good people and of their contribution to the controversy on food values, &c.

SUNDAY SCHOOL WORK.

Conference at Legh Street Primitive Methodist Churgh

FACTORS THAT COUNT.

How to get the young people of the nation into the churches and keep them there is a pro- blem which is causing Sunday school teachers all over the country a great amount of anxiety, and it formed the theme of discussion at a con- ference held in the Legh-street Primitive Metho- dist Chapel on Monday afternoon. The prin- cipal speaker was the Rev. W. Spedding, the general Sunday school secretary of the P.M. Churches in Great Britain, and his visit natur- ally occasioned considerable interest among the local members of the connexion, especially seeing that his office in the denomination placed him in the position of giving first-hand information and advice concerning the subject of discussion. The Rev. W. Clulow, of Golborne, also took part in the conference as a visitor, and embodied many useful hints to Sunday school teachers in a thoughtful paper entitled " The Educational Ideal." The Rev. C. P. Pitt was in the chair, and there was an encouraging attendance. After addressing a few prefatory words of wel- come to the speakers and delegates, The Rev. A. J. Chessum said that there was never greater need for the spiritual education of the child than there was to-day. In War- rington it was pitiful on the Sabbath day to see so many little bairns running about with no one to tell them what to do. The work the churches had to do was tremendous, and they wanted more labourers in the field. If the outcome of that conference was to excite a keener interest in the young people, it would not have been held in vain. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19140919_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
ADVICE TO TEACHERS.

The Rev. W. Clulow said that during a recent annual conference of the National Union of Teachers—day school teachers—at which there were 1,700 delegates, the Archbishop of York declared his educational ideal to be "The fewest possible subjects taught to the smallest possible classes by the best possible teachers." It was worthy of note that his ideal was enthusiastic- ally approved by a large body of the teachers present. For education in its broadest outlook, that was an excellent ideal, and, in his opinion, there was an application of it that might be wisely made to the work of the Sunday school. There was only one recognised text in the Sun- day school—the Bible—so that at least they had the minimum of subjects. But in these days, when it ivas so important, lie might ask, Is the subject before the Sunday schools known by the teacher as it should be?" and also, Does the teacher of to-day love his. subject as he ought?" If the teacher knew his subject well, it would be partly because he loved it so well. It was being emphasised to-day, and rightly so, that it was far more important that the child should love the Bible than that lie should know, it. "He could not love it unless he 'knew it," they might say, but. he (the speaker) could assure them that a child could come to love the Bible because he saw that his parents and his teachers loved it. A child who had learned to love the Bible would not forsake it. On the other hand, when a child 'was compelled to learn the Bible without loving it, he would be glad to free himself from it when he arrived at an age when he could do as he pleased. The responsi- bility resting on the Sunday school teacher to- day was that he should love and know his sub- ject in such a way as to be able to teach it in a manner which would foster in the child a love for the Bible akin to his own. (Hear, hear.) What was needed was more individual training and individual care of the child. That was really imperatively necessary, because children differed so much in age, development, tempera- ment,habit and disposition, that individual treat- ment was demanded. Hence came the necessity for "the smallest possible classes." They were learning to-day that it was necessary for the Sunday school teacher to study the child as well as the Bible, and also that the child should be studied first. Mechanical schemes or a cut- and-dried mode of training would not do. They had relied on that form far too long, and prob- ably that was one explanation of the decrease they mourned in recent years in the number of Sunday school scholars. Personal contact be- tween the teacher and the child was also needed very much, even after the boy or girl had left school. That would tell heavily for the religious awakening of -the child and keep him awake. They had over 7,000,000 young people in the Sunday schools, and if only they could all be won for Christ, they would have a tie.* England in the next generation. However, according to present statistics, they had only one in five of that number who ultimately connected them- selves with the organised Christian Church. If those figures were to be improved upon it would be through a closer and warmer relation- Ship between the teacher and child. The teacher must set a good example to his scholars, for the children had sharper eyes than even Sherlock Holmes was supposed to have. A teacher must also prepare his lessons with great care, for that was imperative for successful teaching. (Applause.) The work of the Sunday school teacher must not be entered upon lightly or ill-advisedly, for it made tremendous de- mands upon the person who took it in hand. No sphere of influence was equal to that of the Sunday school teacher. There was no work that was so intense, so absolute and so decisive. If the work was to be more fruitful and less irksome and common-place, they must have a larger and truer conception of it; they must strive for the ideal and for perfection, for it was not the will of the Father that one of the little ones should perish.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
DO NOT TERRORISE.

The Rev. W. Spedding Said that in the Sunday. school movement they aimed at ideals but had not 'attained them as yet. The idea was to get the teachers to aim for the high ideals and try to get as near them as they possibly could. He supposed they would not realise the ideal, for the ideal of to-day was the actual of to-morrow. The State, in her desire for higher education in things secular had made the pace for them, and the churches were following on with high ideals in religious education in the Sunday school. They should make the young people as conversant as ever they could with the Scriptures. They should get to know all they could about the Bible. It was the duty of the teacher to instruct the children in the literature and ethics of the Word of God, and also in the moral principles enunciated therein. He did not want to terrorise the children with threats of what would happen to them if they sinned, , but he wanted them to be taught to hate sin, and to know that sin was no mere philosophy, theory or speculation, but a most disastrous fact in human experience. They should instil in the child a hatred of anything wrong or impure. They should also teach the children the meaning of destiny, so far as they understood it, as it was revealed by the New Testament. Parents should help the children at home, and so aid the teacher in his work. Those who laboured in the Sunday schools were much more than mere teachers; they were builders, shapers and makers of the manhood and the womanhood of the age, and he would urge them all to deepen their enthusiasm and interest in their great work. (Applause.) The rev, gentleman con- cluded by proposing a hearty vote of thanks to the previous speaker. Mr. G. Faulkner seconded, and said they could never over-estimate the value of the Sun- day school teacher, and he hoped all the teachers would take fresh heart and go on with their good work. After tea, which was served in the schoolroom, came a further very interesting conference on Sunday school work, in which several members took part.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
A NEW AGE.

There was a large attendance in the evening at a public meeting, when Mr. B. Parkes, who was in the chair, urged the parents to set a good example to their children and by so doing help the teachers. (Hear, hear.) The Rev. W. Spedding, in the course of an interesting address, said that to-day there were evidences of a very real and intellectual interest and enthusiasm in the Sunday School. They were living in a new age in relation to Sunday school work, and everyone was taking a greater interest in the child-life of the country. That could have but one result, the winning of the children for the Church of Christ, and, by that, Church:v ent k enthusiasm which There the uplifting of the nation. (Hear, hear.) To- day, th,7--- fle ehrae had new ideals and with them came dwmasoineotnaewsinidgealelssideof asso- ciated with it than that of Sunday school teaching. They therefore required up-to-date teachers. The old-fashioned teachers were all very well, but they wanted the teachers of to'.

DISEASED POTATOES.

Failure to Notify Wart Leads to a

Fine.

OUGHTRINGTON FARMER SUMMONED

A case of considerable interest to farmers was heard at the Lymm Petty Sessions on Wednes- day, when John Lee, farmer, Dog Farm, Ought- rington, was summoned for that he being the occupier of premises where wart disease in potatoes existed, failed to notify the fact to the Board of Agriculture. Defendant pleaded not guilty. Inspector Snell, of London, who prosecuted on behalf of the Board of Agriculture, said that was the first case in Cheshire-to come before a County Bench. The reason farmers and others who grew potatoes were asked to notify the dis- ease was to prevent the diseased potatoes being carried away and used for seed on other farms, because if they were so used or thrown about on other gardens the disease would spread.

TO KEEP DISEASE WITHIN BOUNDS. ,The Board was trying so far as vos possible to keep the disease within its present bounds. It was a very infectious disease, so that farmers should notify it without delay. The notifica- tion did not entail any hardship on :he farmer, because other kinds of potatoes considered im- mune from the disease could be planted in the same soil. The Board were feeling it very keenly that farmers were not reporting as they should do. He thought he ought to point out that, in considering the case before them that morning, the Bench should bear in mind that they were 'protecting other farmers, which was really the whole point of the Order. Sergeant Worth, of the Lancashire County Constabulary, who is stationed at Cadishead, said that on August 14th he saw a greengrocer's cart hawking potatoes which were very much affected with black scab. The potatoes had a fungus growth upon them, which was easily discernible. Edwin Fyldes, greengrocer, Cadishead, said the defendant was the only farmer from whom he had bought potatoes during the three or four weeks prior to the disease being discovered. Witness met the sergeant in the street and drew his attention to the potatoes, about half of which were affected. He gave the name and address of the defendant, and later witness wrote a postcard telling Mr. Lee that he would require no more potatoes as the previous lot were diseased.

The Clerk: Did you turn the potatoes up?— Witness: Yes.

How many were affected?—Nearly half. This was the first time you had noticed the disease?—Yes; it was the first time I had had that kind of potato.

NO PREVIOUS COMPLAINT.

Cross-examined by defendant; witness said he had never before had occasion to complain about the potatoes. P.C. Jeffs said that on March 29th he deliv- ered a Board of Agriculture circular relating to the disease into the hands of the defendant. He had also placed a large poster relating to the same matter at Heatley Railway Station. Inspector Snell: Then Mr. Lee knew there was such an Order in existence? The Chairman (Mr. A. Watkin) : If he read it

OUGHT TO HAVE KNOWN.

Mr. J. Webb, of Haslingden, Crewe, a sub- inspector of the Board of Agriculture, said he visited the defendant's farm on August 21st and was shown into a shed whilst waiting for the defendant. There he found a lot of diseased potatoes, and, in his opinion, it was evident that the presence of disease was known to the defend- ant. Defendant took him to the portion of 'the farm affected, and witness found that the dis- ease was very prevalent. A Magistrate: Do you think he knew he ought to notify ?—Witness : He ought to have known, as it has been a notifiable disease for some years. In reply to a question by the Clerk, witness said he did not think the Order placed a very big onus on the farmer. A COMMON DISEASE.

Defendant said he was not aware that that disease had to be notified, for it could be found in 90 per cent, of the fields in Cheshire. It wa's ' known as "fungus," and was very common. That was the first complaint he had had, al- though he served nine shops in Cadishead, and he got more for his potatoes than any other farmer. The potatoes in question came from a field near the canal and the fungus was gelm- ally found on wet soil, so that Mr. Fyldes must have had his supply taken from that field. The Bench retired, and, on returning, the Chairman said they had given very careful attention to the case and had decided to con- vict. They were only going to fine the defend- ant one shilling, because the costs were heavy, viz., 245.

SUNDAY SCHOO.L WORK—continued.

day to be as qualified as possible, because the work was so great and serious. (Applause.)

SCHOOLS FULL, CHURCH HALF-EMPTY. The REV. W. Clulow said there were many questions facing the Church at the present time. One was how to maintain the numbers of the Church membership, and another was how to increase their congregations? One of the things they had to grieve over throughout the country, especially in Lancashire, was that while their Sunday schools were full, or nearly SO, their churches were half-empty. They all agreed that something ought to be done to get the children of the Sunday school into the church, although they were not all agreed as to how it should be done, nor did they equally feel the urgency of it. Sunday school work was vital to the chiirch, yet it was being shelved, or else only taken in hand very indifferently. If the 7,000,000 children in the Sunday schools could be won for Christ and His Church, it would save the next generation from many l problems that oppressed the world to-day. As passedit l*as throughle the in Sfiuvned aoyf schoolstl eeultimatelydre n who connected themselves with organised religion. It was a lamentable fact that 80 per cent. of them passed out of the school into Vanity Fair.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
The Four Factors.

When they cii;n—e -tj-ciel-With the problem of getting the child, from the Sunday school into the pew there were four factors to be consid- ered—the minister, the teacher, the parents, and the man already in the pew. There wa4 now , no difficulty with regard to the ministers, and, as to the teachers, it ought to be a rule that every one of them should attend all the church services on the Sabbath Day, and that they, should be responsible for the attendance of the members of their classes. The teacher could procure a pew and sit with his scholars in that pew, and undertake that it should be regularly filled. The parents should exercise more control over the children and set the example of church- going. 'The last factor could easily be over- come. There were cronies in every church, but when there was essential work to be done they could be counted out (Applause.) The Rev. William Reid, the president of the local Sunday School Union, said the work of the Sunday school teacher was a grand thing, and he was glad that their teachers were trying to make themselves as qualified as possible for their work. (Applause.) . The Rev. A. J. Chessum thanked the speakers. During the evening, the choir, under the con- ductorship of Mr. A. Spruce, rendered the anthem "0 taste and see," and Mrs. Hoyle con- tributed a solo.

LYMM.

Well Done!—Lymni has given about 170 of its sons for the defence of the realm. This num- ber includes Reservists, Territorials, and recruits.

On the Bench.—The magistrates at the Lymm Petty Sessions on Wednesday were Messrs. Alfred Watkin (in the chair),, A. W. Callender, J. Coxon, 11. Holden, S. W. Gould and T. H. Warburton.

A Helping Hand.—Mr. F. Evans, the Cross, Lymm, has been appointed honorary secretary of the Soldiers' and Sailors' Families' Associa- tion, and lie asks for the names of dependents of men who are serving the country. This association and the Relief Committee are to meet weekly.

Must Appear in Person.—Three defendants, charged with ,riding cycles without lights, failed to answer their summonses at the Lymm Petty Sessions, and instead forwarded letters. The Chairman, in intimating that the cases would be adjourned so that defendants could be pres- ent, said people were far too fond of that kind of thing—sending notes.

Football.—On Wednesday next the Lymqi shop assistants' football team will be th, visi- tors to Marple to contest a Manchester Wednes- day League fixture. Ike first match of the season was played on September 9th, when the Lymm team suffered a five-nil defeat at Stock- port. The club secretary is Mr. W. H. Blount, the treasurer Mr. W. Richards, and the captain and sub-captain Messrs. G. Copeland and F. Guest respectively. Headmaster and Soldier.—Owing to the head- master at the Lymm Grammar School being an officer in a Works Company of the Royal Engineers, he had to mobilise with his regiment at the outbreak of war and leave his duties at the school. This placed the Governors in a very awkward position, but happily all difficulty has been removed. By special leave from the War Office, Mr. Hawkins is permitted to tem- porarily resume his post at the school. The Headmaster has been in the Royal Engineers since the inauguration of the Territorial Force.

After an Argument.—At the Lymm Petty Ses- sions on Wednesday, George Moston, a Lymm man, was fined 20s. including costs, for assault- ing John Boniton, a barber, employed by Mr. Marsh, barber, of Lymm. The evidence showed that the quarrel occurred in the Bull's Head public-house, after an argument about Chester and the Duke of Westminster. Complainant kept repeating that he was a better Englishman than the defendant, but this was said after de- fendant had made the remark that Poulton was a foreigner.—Mr. Nichols, licensee of the hotel, said he was surprised to find defendant in that position. He saw him strike Boulton.

Called to the Colours.—The 21 young men from Lymm who—following a recruiting meeting at which the Rev. William Bracecarnp was the speaker—joined Lord Kitchener's Army at Ches- ter last week, were sent home on ten days' leave. However, on Sunday evening Mr. F. Evans re- ceived word that the men had to be dispatched to Chester as early as possible, so with the help of Mr. G. R, Williamson the recruits were in- formed and sent to Chester about noon the following day. One of the recruits, taking advantage of the leave of absence, had repaired ' to North Wales for the week-end, but he was communicated with and he made his way to join his comrades.

With the Colours.—At the Lymm Petty Ses- sions on Wednesday a summons against Dudley Aukland, of St. Helens, for riding a motor cycle and sidecar at High Legh on June 14th without a red rear-light, was withdrawn, on the application of Superintendent Sutherland.— The case had been before the Court twice pre- viously and at the last hearing it was stated that defendant had joined his regiment, and the magistrates asked for inquiries to be made a to the accuracy of that statement.—Superin- tendent Sutherland now said he had been in communication with the Chief Constable of St. Helens and had received a. reply to the effect that defendant was an officer in. the Territorial,s and had gone with hie regiment upon their being mobilised.—On hearing that, the magistrates agreed to the withdrawal of the summons.

For Home Defence.—A Home Defence Corps has been organised in the Lymm district, and is meeting with success, for, up to date, about 150 men are drilling two or three nights a week. The Oughtrington and Heatley squad has been putting in three drills a week in the Oughtring- ton covered tennis vourt, whilst in Lymm the volunteers have been under instruction two nights weekly, but for the future the Lymm men will drill on Tuesday and Thursday even- ings (8.30) and Saturday afternoons (3.30), at the Drill Hall and in Mr. Johnson's field if fine. No man under 35- years of age is admitted into the corps, this step being taken to prevent the organisation clashing with recruiting. The drill instructors are Mr. W. K. Kershaw, Mr. G. H. Williamson and Mr. Wilcox, and Mr.. G. A. Cook is the secretary. At a recent meeting Mr. F. Evans was added to the committee..

===== 0002601_19140919_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
ADULTERATED MILK.

Peculiar Petty Sessions Case Dismissed

SYMPATHY WITH DEFENDANT.

At the Lymm Petty Sessions on Wednesday morning, William Moss, Great Oak Farm, High Legh, was summoned at the instance of William James HaHard, weights and measures inspector, of Chester, for selling milk, through a servant or agent, not of the substance or quality de- manded, inasmuch as the milk fat was less than three per cent. Mr. T. S. Steel, solicitor, Warrington, de- fended, and formally pleaded not guilty on behalf of his client. _ _

Inspector "iialla-;:d. said he was in Eagle Brow on the 12th of last month, When he saw a milk float belonging to defendant. He stopped the driver and asked for a pint of milk, and, when served, he placed it in three bottles, one of which was sent to the analyst and one to de- fendant, witness retaining the remaining one. Witness explained to the driver that the milk would be analysed. Subsequently when lie re- ceived information that the milk was below the standard, he visited the defendant's farm and asked Mr. Moss if he could offer any explana- tion. Defendant asked witness if it was late when he took the sample, and, he replied that it was. Defendant then stated that this man told him that he had run short of milk, and had bought some, but, as a matter of fact, that which was purchased was separated milk, and would account. for the deficiency in the fat.

No Knowledge of Adulteration. Cr‘os-examined by -Mr. Steel, witness agreed that defendant stated that the milk supplied to his man was as it came from the cow. During the conversation did defendant call the man in?—Yes. And did the man admit that he had, without Mr. Moss knowing it, bought separated milk and put it into the tankard ?—Yes. At the time you took the samples 'aid the man say anything about it?—No. Having regard to the conversation with Mr. Moss and the admissions made by his man, have you any reason to think Mr. Moss had any know_ ledge of the adulteration?—No. In answer to the Clerk, witness said the mini.

mum of milk fat should not be lower than three per cent. - -

Extraordinary Case. Mr. Steel, addressing the Bench, said the case was a most extraordinary one, such a one as he had not been in before, and probably one the like of which the magistrates had never experi- enced before. The analyst's certificate and the statement made by the inspector could not be disputed, but he thought the Bench would agree when they had heard the case, that Mr. Mpss was entirely absolved from any knowledge of the adulteration. The defendant was a highly re- spected farmer in High Legh, who, in addition to carrying on the business of his farm, had a milk round, and had enjoyed a reputation for supplying excellent milk. Sometimes it was im- possible to exactly gauge the requirements of the customers, so the defendant took the pre- caution to supply his man with money, so that, in the event GI his running short of milk, more could be bought from farmers or milk dealers close at hand. The man had also received in- structions that when milk had to be purchased it had not to be mixed with defendant's milk.

Bought Separated Milk.

On he morning in question the man ran short of milk, but, instead of buying from a farmer or dealer, he bought from a man who skimmed his milk and afterwards sold it as food for pigs. This was how defendant's servant got the separ- ated milk; he bought some six quarts. De- fendant would have known nothing about this had not the inspector taken the sample, when the man, thinking something further would be heard of the matter made an admission of what he had done.

That case had revealed a very great deal to the defendant. He had found things out about the man he had never dreamed of before. He discovered, for instance, that the man engaged in betting transactions, and called at public- houses, and, in making a comparison between the book and the quantity of milk taken out and that returned, deficiences were revealed. The defendant very much regretted that the case had come forward. It had given him very great pain that he should have been summoned at all.

Mr. Steel's Suggestion. The offence was quite contrary to the defend- ant's wishes, for he would be the last person in the world to countenance such a thing. Mr. Moss could not immediately dispense with his servant's services, because he was the only man who knew the round, but when it had been learned by another man, the servant had been discharged. Mr. Steel, in concluding, said he knew that, technically, Mr. Moss was at fault. If it had been possible to have issued a sum- mons against the servant he would have advised it, but the law made it clear that the master was responsible. A similar case had been de- cided, and if the magistrates were satisfied that the offence was the deliberate act of the servant, apart from the knowledge of the master, it could be taken into consideration in mitigation of the offence. He suggested that the case be dismissed on payment of costs. , The magistrates retired, and on their return, the Chairman (Mr. A. Watkin) said they be- lleved every word Mr. Steel had said, and sym- pathised with Mr. Moss. They were glad to be able to do as Mr. Steel asked, and to dismiss the case on payment of costs.

STOCKTON HEATH

Parish Council.—Councillor Mclntyre presided at the meeting of the Parish Council held on Tuesday evening, and thre was a good attend- ague of members.

Precept Met.—The precept issued on the over- seers for ...tl5 has been met. Such was the report of the clerk at the Parish Council meeting on Tuesday.

Autumn Show Abandoned.—On account of the war, Mr. White, the secretary of the Garden Society, has issued a notice to the effect that the usual Autumn Show will be abandoned this year.

Police Station's Belated Spring Cleaning.— Painters and decorators Are hard at work on the interior of the Police Station and the result promises to be most effective.

Present at the Parochial Committee.—At a meeting of the Stockton Heath Parochial Com- mittee on Tuesday there were present Council- lors Pilling (presiding), Westbrook, Whitley, J. Hallows, C. W. Hallows, Mclntyre, McDermid, Dukes, Rose, Clarke, Pearson and Warburton, with Messrs. W. H. Warburton (acting clerk), S. Wright (surveyor), and E. Bradshaw (clerk to the Parish Council).

Alleged Obstructive Hedgerow.—lt was re- ported at the Parochial Committee meeting on Tuesday that the matter of the obstruction alleged to be caused to, motorists coming down London-road and Grappenhall-road by the high hedge surrounding the Hall Cafe Gardens was receiving the attention of those responsible, and also of the County Surveyor.—ln regard to the complaint as to the flooding of one end of War- burtok-street, it was reported at the same meet- ing that the Highways Surveyor had communi- cated with each of the owners and conveyed the Council's opinion to them.

Human and Equine Tree Damagers.—At a meeting of the Parish Council on Tuesday, Councillor Whitley made a complaint that since the last meeting further damage had been done to trees by horses. There had been a case in Gaskell-street.—Councillor Westbrook: Send the horses to the war. (Laughter.)—The Chairman: We cannot do anything beyond reporting the matter to the police.—Councillor J. Hallows : I know of a case in which a young man has stripped some bark off a tree in West-avenue.— The Chairman: Ho you know himF—Councillor Hallows: No.—The appointment of someone to take care of the trees was left in the hands of the Clerk and the Chairman.

An Awkwardly Placed Lamp.—At the Parochial Committee meeting on Tuesday Coun- cillor Westbrook mentioned the awkward posi- tion of the lamp at the corner of Whitefield- road and Red-lane Bridge. There had been an accident there recently, but he did not mean to infer that the accident was caused by the lamp being fixed in the place it was. However, any- one riding down there saw the lamp in the way, and the only means ty which they could see anything else properly was by getting on one side of the lamp, near the wall. He would like to see the lamp moved a little to one side.— Councillor Whitley: It needs one on the other side.—The Chairman: Will you look into that, Councillor Whitley, as chairman of the Light- ing Committee?—Councillor Whitley replied that the matter would have the prompt attention of his committee.

Revision Court.—For the purpose of revising the list of the electors in the Appleton, Grap- penhall and Stockton Heath polling districts, which comprise the following parishes: Apple- ton with Hull and Stretton, Grappenhall and Latchford Without, Stockton Heath, Walton Inferior, Walton Superior, and that part of Warrington within the geographical county of Chester, Mr. Clement Elphinstone Lloyd attended at the Stockton Heath Police Court yesterday. Mr. W. Cliffe represented the Liberals, and the Conservative interests were watched by Mr. James Duckworth. The Assistant Overseers present were: Grappenhall, Mr. C. Holt; Latchford Without, Mr. J. Smith; Stockton Heath, Mr. E. Bradshaw; Warrington and Walton Inferior, Mr. H. Lawrence; Walton Superior, Mr. A. Moulsdale. There were also present Councillor Pilling (chairman of the Parochial Committee) and Councillor J. Whit- ley.—The business was of a purely formal nature and the Court rose shortly after noon.

"BUSINESS AS USUAL." Parish Meetings To Be Held Despite War.

The half-yearly meeting of parishioners will be held on Monday, October sth. This decision was arrived at after a short discussion at the Parish Council meeting on Tuesday, at which Councillor Westbrook moved that they should I make the war another excuse—that having re- gard to it they should not hold a meeting this half-year. Councillor Dukes: Does the war interfere with parish work? Councillor Westbrook: If it does not, why not? It is interfering with various things. A lady even alleged that her hen had been so dis- turbed by the war that it had ceased to lay eggs. (Laughter.) Councillor Dukes: I don't think we ought to trouble about cackle of that kind. (Laughter.) Let us go on with our business. Councillor 111cDermid : We should leave the ratepayers to decide as t the non-continuance of the meetings, and not take the matter into our own hands. Councillor Dukes: We don't know what may arise. We may have to consider further provi- sion for the relief of distress and other questions of that kind. Councillor Westbrook: You could call a special meeting. Councillor Dukes: Why take steps, at this juncture, to defer a meeting which should be held in the middle of October, when things are so much in the melting-pot? The Acting Clerk: It is a question of date now. Councillor Westbrook: I withdraw. lam penitent. (Laughter.)

FIRE-BOX AS SMOKE-ROOM. Suggested Rendezvous for the Old Men

The fire-box, over which there has been so much talk, is to be abandoned, for at a meeting of the Parochial Committee on Tuesday Coun- cillor McDermid (chairman of the Fire Engine Committee) reported that in reference to the proper housing of the fire appliances, it had been decided by his committee that part of the prem- ises of Mr. James Baguley be taken for one year at an inclusive rent of 2s. 6d. per week, the land- lord to pay the rates and the structural altera- tions necessary to be carried out by the Council. They also recommended that the fixing of the fire bell on the Police Station premises be pro- ceeded with at once. The box had always been very small for its purpose and did not hold all the appliances. The Chairman: Will the new place be easy to get at? _ . _

- Councillor McDermid : Yes; I think it will be very suitable for the purpose. I propose the adoption of the minutes and that the surveyor be empowered to dispose of the old box and carry out the necessary alterations. Councillor J. Hallows: There are two or three of us who have been looking for a convenient place in which the old gentlemen in the village might spend their afternoons and nights when it is stormy. The Chairman: The proposal is not seconded. A Member: I second. Councillor Hallows; We have been trying to find a place where the old men can smoke their pipes and have a rest. The thought struck me that this place would do until we got a more suitable one wherein the old Ten could have a smoke and talk over the wok news. I only throw the suggestion out. I have offered my greenhouse for the purpose, but it is too far for them to come. I suggest that the old box would be a suitable place for the present. It could be locked up at night and the old men could have the key. Councillor Westbrook: The place would only

• E.,••

make a decent habitation for a St. Bernard dog to live in Properly.

Councillor -Mcnermid: Might I suggest that the surveyor dispose of the box to the best ad- vantage?_.

Councillor Pearson: There would be no objec- tion to Councillor Hallows's suggestion. Councillor Rose: I am entirely in agreement with Councillor Hallows. It is really a pity to see these men wandering about with nowhere to go, and I think we could find a suitable place at small cost that would be of great advantage. Councillor Clarke: They are not like Bel- gians, without hearth and home. Councillor Hallows: Couldn't you make-us a present of it or lend it to us? There is another place in Derby-road, which is very central. The Chairman: They would not go to it.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
GRAPPENHALL.

District Nurse and the Red Cross Society.— Owing to Miss Callender having been appointed , the matron at the Red Cross Hospital at Raddon I Court, the village of Grappenhall is left without its district nurse. A meeting of subscribers to the District Nursing Fund was held on Satur- day night at Mr. Roger C. Parr's house, Mr. Parr presiding over a small attendance. The Chairman said the committee were unanimously of the opinion that the nurse should be paid her full salary whilst working in the Red Cross hospital, but they thought it advisable to call a meeting of subscribers in case there were objec- tions. Whilst Miss Callender was in the hos- pital the village would be without a nurse, for they could not provide another, as they were all engaged owing to the European trouble. It was, however, quite possible that the hospital would not be used, and in that event the nurse would not leave them. She would receive no pay for her services at the hospital. It was agreed to pay her full salary during the time she might be away on Red Cross duties.

came on with great determination, and they were awarded a free-kick for obstruction. Bland took it, but shot wide of the mark. The pres- sure on the Warrington citadel still continued, and some warm work was put in by both sides. A strenuous effort by 'Brookes, who found touch in the Broughton half, temporarily relieved the situation, the Warrington front rank rising to the occasion and giving the visitors a busy Minute or two. Several scrums took place, and the homesters struggled desperately to turn the situation to their advantage, but eventually the visiting threequarters opened out the play and embarked upon 'a dangerous raid into Warring- ton territory., They were giving nothing away, and things looked black for the homesters, when Morgan dashed in and saved brilliantly. But the relief was only temporary, for Barnes, get- ting possession after Danson had found touch well down the field, crossed the line, and despite the embarrassing attentions of several of the Warrington men, succeeded in scoring. Barlow took the kick, but on this occasion failed. Spurred on to greater effort by this set-back, the Warrington pack worked hard and made some headway, but their most strenuous efforts failed to provide any work, on their behalf, for the scorer. The arrival of the interval found the teams engaged on the half-way line, the score reading :—Broughton Bangers, 1 goal 2 tries (8 points); Warrington, 1 goal 1 try (5 points).

===== 0002601_19140919_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
HOT WORK OPENS SECOND HALF.

Almbst immediately after the crossing over Jones andlßland got in some hot work, but the Warrington defence proved equal to the occa- sion, and soon the tables were turned, the homestera attacking, with force, though nothing came of their promising onslaught. For a few minutes play was of a give-and-take character, Jolley distinguishing himself by some good, clean kicking. Dainteth also came into pro- minence, a piece of dribbling work which he put in being greatly to the tastr, of the crowd, who applauded it most heartily. He got past several ,men, but the visitors' last line proved invulnerable. Jolley was also playing a fine game at this stage, figuring auspiciously in more than one difficult situation. Although in arrears on the half-time balance sheet, War- rington were by. no means disheartened, and were putting up a first-class battle at this stage, availing themselves of 'pretty nearly every op- portunity that offered. Thomas and Ti.santer initiated a move that promised much, but, un- happily, it proved to be another case in which promise and performance did not square, though the two players named gave the visitors a warm time of it while it lasted. They relied on sheer weight to effect their purpose, but they were too well attended to and were forced to abandon their enterprise, just when, from their point of view, things were looking rosy. Temporarily plaecentred in the home territory and was not of a particularly interesting character, but the Warrington threequarter line infused some ex- citement into it by indulging in a splendid pas- sing run, which roused the onlookers to a high pitch of excitement. Nothing tangible came of it, however, Danson being fully prepared and applying the check in the nick of time.

WARRINGTON'S. HOPES RAISED HIGH. During the brisk tussle which ensued, Barlow, who had been distinguishing himself by some specially vigorous work, was hurt, and had to retire. Undismayed by the temporary loss of one of their handiest men, the Rangers still pressed, and the defenders had all their work cut out to deal with them. At this juncture both sides were contesting the issue for all they were worth, and excitement ran very high. Tackling was vigorous and entirely to the taste of the crowd. Barnes got in some smart drib- bling work for Broughton, but it was rendered abortive before he became really dangerous. During the progress of some brisk mid-field work Horrigan received an intdi7 to the head that necessitated his retirement, but he was not absent for long, resuming with his brow swathed in bandages. The visitors kept up an incessant pressure, and Bland made a mark near the War- rington goal, but Barlow failed to convert. Some scrappy play ensued, bit the teams soon pulled themselves together, and the interest of the spectator which had been waning during a period of monotony, was roused to fever heat as Laws and Brookes indulged in a splendid run which raised the high hopes of the Warrington supporters. Nothing, however, came of their effort, the Broughton ,defenders bringing them up. Jolley was obstructed, but no advanta,ge came frtrm the free kick, Brookes just failing.

AN EXCITING STRUGGLE. Shortly afterwards an exciting struggle was witnessed near the Warrington line. Barnes and Bland rushed .the ball up and it looked as if they would be over, having successfully eluded most of the defenders, when they were brought to a full-stop, and Laws, with almost a clear course in front of him, ran the ball out of play. Hereabouts Broughton were -doing most of the attacking, but Warrington occasionally got a look-in. Baker made one particularly promis- ing run on their behalf, but though he was fairly well backed up, the homesters seemed unable to make any impression upon the visit- ors' defence, though it is true that Danson was forced to kick into touch to avert possible danger. Tranter, too, was responsible for a nice bit of individual work, which did not escape the notice of the onlookers. lie found touch in the Broughton "25." Dainteth was also prominent for a smart effort, which took the form of one of the neatest dribbling feats of the afternoon. Jolley was tackled whilst in possession, and the Warrington threequarters took the ball down- field in spanking style, but Danson pulled them , up before they were able to turn their effort to good account. Broughton indulged in a spell 1 of attacking, but they were successfully held in check.

WARRINGTON GAME TO THE END. The hopes of the spectators were raised when Young picked up the ball from a scrum and made off ' in fine style, passing to Laws when hotly pressed. But such hopes were dispelled when Lfiws was brought down by Jones with a fine tackle. Still, Warrington were not discour- aged when effort after effort faired, but perse- vered to the bitter end, and in the closing stages of the game they gave every bit as much as they received. When Young, with Dainteth close on his heels, initiated a swift movement, it looked as if the equalising points were about to arrive, but the opposing forces proved .too strong, and Young was forced to part to Brookes, who was summarily brought to ground by Denson. Broughton then took a turn at attacking, and. Barnes was responsible for a splendid piece of dribbling work. A penalty punt by Brookes found the cross-bar and the ball rebounded into the hands of three of the home team, but they could not make anything of the chance. Just afterwards Warrington were awarded another penalty kick, but no advantage came of it, Brookes driving too hard. In the last few min- utes Warrington did most of the pressing and more than once they gave reason for hoping that they might put themselves on equal terms with Broughton, but it was not to be, and when time

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 19, 1914.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW THE PLAYERS PLAYED.

The Warrington pack worked in a Manner which left very: little room for serious grumb- ling. Though beaten they certainly did' their best, and no doubt in the near future they will make up to their supporters for this ttmporary check in their onward career. Jolley played a good,' sound game. His kicking was strong, but he Was a trifle at fault on one or two occasions when it came to catching. The thlrequarter line, including even the captain, Brookes, were a trifle disappointing, their passing frequently being at fault, as the result of which more than one chance was lost. Morgan was a little slow on one or two occasions when brisk action was called for, and Baker let more than a single opportunity slide, whilst Laws displayed a diffi- culty in holding the ball. Dainteth and Young played useful football, and, perhaps, were "bright, particular stars." Tranter, Fearnley and Clare were the pick of the forward line, and when opportunity offered they proved that they were not lacking in either initiative or dash. Danson, the visitors' full-back, did not let Jolley shine alone in his department, but was always alert, safe and clean. Bland and Horrigan—especially the latter, who made some brilliant individual runs—were "mart among a set of threequarters that worked with cohesion and a purpose. Jones at half-back did well.

It was not Warrington's day—the players were outweighed on a heavy day, which suited the visitors to a nicety. However, weather is part of the game, and we must hope for better con- ditions when the teams next meet.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON "A " WIN. Combination Points Lifted at

Broughton.

Warrington "A" made some slight amends for the defeat inflicted on their first team on Saturday by taking the Combination points off Broughton “A," whom they visited on the Cliffe ground. Despite wretched weather, a good- sized crowd witnessed the match. At the last moment an alteration was Made in the War- rington team; Logan taking a place`in the 'for- ward line, vice Green, who was originally selected. The rain and wind were all against a classy exhibition being given and made handling the ball a difficult matter, with the result that more dribbling was seen than is usual, in a Northern Union game. Play ruled pretty even throughout the first half and was not without its exciting moments, some pretty tar.kling being witnessed on both sides. Twice during the first few minutes Warrington looked like getting over, but their hopes were dashed to the ground just at the crucial moment. Indeci- sion lost them a good opportunity and one the like of which did not come their -way again throughout the opening moiety. The opening points fell to Broughton when the game was only a few minutes old, Hayes being responsible for a dashing run through, which he finished up in fine style by registering a 'try, which, how- ever, went unconverted. ; IThere was no other score in the first half, which ended with the hornesters,in the enjoyment of ti 3-nil lead.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0137.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON WAKE UP.

......... . In the second half the Warrington men be- stirred themselves to good purpose, and, aided by a slight wind, had most of the play. They attacked desperately on several occasions, but, in the main, found the Broughton defence a pretty solid one. Only twice did they manage 1 to pierce it, although they subjected it to hard pressure for the greater part of the moiety. After a skilful bit of work Harmer saw his opportunity, and, seizing it, was over the line, although desperate efforts were made to stop him. It was an exciting piece of work and was appreciated after the usual manner by the somewhat attenuated, but nevertheless entliu- siastic, crowd. Taberner took the kick, but shot wide. The game had only been going again for a few minutes when Harold Davis, running up from the threequarter line, gave a smart ex- hibition of dodging work and registered a second try for Warrington. Again Taberner took the kick,-'and again he failed. Nothing else of note marked the game, which therefore ended in a win for the visitors by two tries to one.

HOW THEY PLAYED. On the whole, and despite the handicap caused by the wretched weath4r and the any- thing but ideal condition of the turf, Warring- ton played a sound game; indeed at times their display almost deserved the description of bril- liant and would not have disgraced the first team. The result hardly accurately indicated the respective merits of the sides, Warrington deserving to win by a bigger margin, and they would have done had they been able to co- operate so well together in the first half as they did in the second. Bramhall specially distin- guished himself by his work in the rear of the Warrington pack, and Harmer and Mather were the pick of the forwards, both playing a very keen game.

This match, which was played on-the Gorsey- lane enclosure on Saturday, ended, in an easy victory, for Rylands',•despite the fact that they fielded only twelve men. SOME INTEREJTING LEAGUE RESULTS.

On Saturday football was played under very adverse conditions, a drizzling rain falling during the whole time the games were in pro- gress: However, all the games set down for decision in the Warrington and District League were brought to a satisfactory conclusion. Fair- field ' United were visitors to the Central and defeated them in a very decisive manner by 5 goals to 1, which goes to prove that the United have got together a very good team this season. . _ _ . .

The Elmwood Cons, travelled to Earlestown to meet the Y.M.C.A. teatn, and returned defeated by 5 goals to 2. On their present form the Cons. will have to pull themselves together if they intend to gather points. Last week their de- fenders gave a very poor display in the second portion of the game. Norke, in goal, was at fault with the first goal, but later in the game he made several good saves. Phcenix, at centre- half, played a strenuous game throughout. The Latchford Baptists opened their cam- paign by opposing Hollins Green on the Lower Wash-lane ground, and although they had to acknowledge defeat by the odd goal in five, they were by no means disgraced. The game proved very interesting, arid was keenly contested, the weather conditions being more suitable to the visitors, who were the heavier set of players, and also had the advantage accruing from hav- ing played together in a previous game: The Baptists had the services of Stubbs, formerly of the Latchford A.F.C., who scored both goals for the home team, one from a penalty and the other from a free kick just outside the penalty area. • •

Th 6 Fairfield A.F.C. had Paddington as oppo- nents, and the home club at the interthl were four goals in arrears, but after turning round they made strenuous efforts and were successful in lowering the Paddington citadel on three occasions, but the visitors' defence played a stee- ling game, and the home forwards failed to get on equal terms and had to retire defeated by the odd goal in seven. Orford White Star had Bank Quay Fees, as visitors, and a strenuous game was witnessed. The home team were unfortunate in having to retire defeated by the odd goal of the match.

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT LEAGUE.

EARLESTOWN Y.M.C.A. V. ELM WOOD CONS. , These teams met on Saturday in the above competition, on the Y.M.C.A. ground. The weather conditions were not favourable to good football. The following players turned out, in charge of Mr. J. Smith, referee : Y.M.C.A.: E. Martin; Atkinson and Pope; Fairhurst, H. Martin and Buckley; W. Taylor, Hughes, Riley, Gilman and Lee. Elmwood Cons.: Norke; Joe Phoenix and Millar; Pritchard, Jim Phoenix and Dutton; W. Phoenix, Ellam, Mellor, Wells and Farrar. The visitors won the toss and Riley set the ball in motion, and the homesters at once in- vaded the visitors' quarters and Phoenix and Millar had to clear hurriedly. From another attack the homesters gained a corner-kick, when Riley headed over the bar. The visitors trans- ferred play to the opposite end, and the final effort of their attack went! wide. During an- other attack Pope kicked through his own goal, thus giving the visitors the lead. Encouraged by this success the visitors gained another cor- ner, but failed to turn it to account. The home players now improved and the game was con- tested in the visitors' half. Good passing by the home forwards enabled Riley to head the ball towards goal. Norke missed badly and allowed the visitors to equalise, and shortly afterwards Riley again did the needful from a well-placed corner-kick. Even play followed in mid-field, until Pritchard got away and sent in a long dropping shot which beat Martin and passed just under the bar. Both teams ,ere now playing a keen game, but nothing further had been scored when the interval arrived, each side having two goals to their credit. On resuming, the home players soon settled down to their game, and Gilman beat Norke with a beauty. This reverse seemed .to dis- hearten the visitors, and they were penned to their own half for the best part of the remaiMler of the game. Riley scored a fourth goal for the home team, which was quickly followed by a fifth from Gilman. The visitors made a few spasmodic rushes towards the home goal, but were easily held in check, and when the final whistle sounded the --suit was: Y.M.C.A., 5 goals; Elmwood Com , goals.

LATCHFORD BAPTISTS V. HOLLINS GREEN

- - - 'The Baptists opened their season's fixtures with a visit from the Hollins Green Club. The unfavourable con- dgaitnlicie wns."Tsht:rlted underiomecaptavienr Won the toss. The visitors set the ball rolling and were soon in the home quarters, but were met with a pair of resolute backs. The visitors continued to have the best of the game and were dangerous when near goal. Adams accepted a pass with alacrity and opened the score for the visitors, the first point being quickly .added to by a good shot from Atkinson. Following these reverses the homesters made strenuous efforts, and put the visitors on the defence. The ball was handled inside the area. Stubbs took the kick and registered a point for his team, thus reduc- ing the lead. But the visitors came up very strongly once more and Perrins scored a third goal with a fast, low shot, which completely beat the home goalie. The score at half-time was in favour of the visitors by 3 goals to_l.

On the resumption, the home team played with more dash, but owing to over-eagerness missed Several good opportunities of scoring. The visitors did not play quite as well as in the first .portion, being kept more on the defensive. From a free-kick just outside the penalty area Stubbs scored the best goal of the match for the home team. Play was continued chiefly in the vis'tors' half, but their defence held out, and at the call of time the result was :-ITollins Green, 3 goals; Latchford Baptists, 2 goals.

FREE ADMISSION FOR SOLDIERS 'AND SAILORS.

The Warrington Football Club announce that all members subscribing at the low rate of sub- scription, called up or volunteering their ser- vices for the war, will, on their return, be re- admitted at the same rate. Soldiers and sailors in uniform will be admitted free to ground and stands during the war.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
FAIRFIELD V. PADDINGTON.

The first-named club opened their home fix- tures in the League with the last-named as visitors to Padgate-lane. The Paddington cap- tain was successful in the spin of the coin, and the home team started the game under very unfavourable climatic conditions. The visitors began with only ten players, but directly after the kick-off they made a spirited attack on the home goal, but the Fairfield backs relieved finely. The visitors showed better form, and were having the best of the exchanges, and after five minutes' play they gained the lead. Following this success even play ensued for a time, after which the visitors returned to the attack and were soon rewarded ;with a second point. The play continued to run in the visitors' favour, and further goals were added, and at the interval they held a lead of 4 clear goals. On resuming, the home team showed up to better advantage, and from a strong attack the visitors conceded a corner, which was well placed, and the home team scored their first point. Encouraged by this success, they played with more confidence and kept their opponents on the defensive, and, at length, from a long shot, which the visitors' goalkeeper judged badly, the home team secured their second goal. At this point the homesfers were _all over their opponents and kept up a strong attack. The visitors' goalie allowed the ball to pass between his legs into the net, a third goal being thus registered by the home team. This reverse nettled the visitor S and they infused desperate energy into their play, the homesters in turn going at it for all they were worth. But they failed to get on equal terms, and when the final whistle was blown the result was in favour of the visitors, viz.: Paddington, 4 goals; Fairfield, • 3 goals.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON CENTRAL v. FAIRFIELD

UNITED.

These teams met in Victoria Park on Satur- day and played in a steady fall of rain through- out the game. Each side was a man short. The United, winning the toss, decided to play 'with the slope in their favour., The Opening ex- changes were very evenly contested, and each goal was visited. At the end of 15 minutes' play the United made their team complete and, then They gradually gained, the upper hand and captured the homesters' citadel on three occa- sions in quick succession. The home team could not penetrate the visitors' defence, and at the interval the United were leading by 4 goals to none. On the resumption, the home team were still minus a player, but the game was more evenly contested, and following strong attacks on both goals, each team were, in turn, awarded penalty kicks, both of which were converted. At the call of time the score .board read :—Fairfield United, 5 goals; Warrington Central, 1 goal.

ORFORD WHiTE STAR v. BANK QUAY RECS.

These teams met on the ground of the first- named club, adjoining Gorsey-lane, and, owing to the referee being detained, a late start was made. The homesters had made some changes in their team since the previous week and gave an improved exhibition. The first half was keenly contested, both teams being evenly matched. But, owing to the greasy nature of the turf, both sets of forwards missed oppor- tunities of scoring, and, at the interval, neither side had registered a point. After the change of ends both teams played a strenuous game, and at length the Recs. were successful in gaining the lead. The home team strained every nerve to draw level with their opponents—Whose backs defended stoutly—and just before the call of time they put tte ball between the posts, but the goal was disallowed, owing to the offside rule being broken. When the final whistle sounded the score read :—Bank Quay Recs., I goal; Orford White Star, nil.

IN THE COURTS—continued.

THURSDAY. Before Mr. W. Roberts (in the chair), Dr. Naden, and Mr. W. T. Williams. A Story With Two Sides To It—Walter Laly, 16, Ship-yard, again Appeared before the Bench, and the Clerk explained that he had been re- manded from the previous day on a charge of causing grievous bodily harm to his wife in a Ship-yard lodging-house by pushing her down.— Supt. Williamson said that on Tuesday evening Detective-Sergeant Reddington received a com- plaint in Bridge-street and went to the lodging- house, 16, Ship-yard. The woman was lying on the floor, suffering from a severe wound in the head. All the people in the house were the worse for drink, and the woman said her hus- band had hit her with his fists. She was taken to the Infirmary and her husband was arrested. . The people in the lodging-house were inter- viewed and two of these said the woman had fallen, but one old man said that the prisoner had knocked his wife down on the fender. The woman stated now that she did not remember anything, and the only thing the police could do in the circumstances was to offer no evidence.— . The woman said that her husband never struck her. She fell in the street many a time because her head was bad. She occasionally felt and looked drunk, even if she had not had any for weeks. She would bet that she looked drunk that morning.—The Chairman: The case is very suspicious, and I would advise you (prisoner) to be more careful. The case will be with- drawn.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0149.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE COURTS.

BOROUGH POLICE COURT.

SATURDAY.

Before Mr. R. T: Fairclough (in the chair), Alderman Tinnion, Messrs. T. Pemberton, A. J. Jolley and J. Upson. A Lad's Thefts.—Harry Bennett (17), 5, Tork- ington-court, Brinksway, Stockport, was charged on remand with stealing on August 26th from the dwelling-house, 23, Myrtle-grove, Warring- ton, one overcoat, two jackets, one vest, one cap and one muffler value 305., the property of Wil- liam Nixon and others.—Supt. Williamson said the boy had been lodging with the Nixons, and early on the morning of August 26th he got up early to go to work, and later, when a member of the family went downstairs, he noticed that the articles had been taken. It was found that the lad had been tramping the country for over six months, and that he had been arrested at Stockport on September 4th. His mother gave him a bad character. He (the Superintendent) asked for a remand until Friday, so that the boy could be sent to Liverpool with a view to his being got into a Borstal institution.—The remand was granted.—The lad was again brought up on Friday morning, when the magistrates present on the Bench were Mr. S. Wright (in the chair), Aldermen Pierpoint, Bolton and Smethurst, and Messrs. G. Pilling, A. J. Jolley, W. Furness and T. Locker.-:-Superintendent Williamson informed the Court that the prison authorities reported that the lad was a suitable case for Borstal treatment, and he was now brought before the Court with a view to his being committed to the Liverpool Quarter Ses- sions, so that be might be sent to a Borstal institution.—Additional evidence was taken.— Mrs. Eliza Hudson, a second-hand dealer, of 47, Church-street, Warrington, said that the lad came to her shop, and, saying that he was going to enlist, sold her some of the stolen articles for is. 6d. Later on she handed them over to D.S. Reddington. Before buying, she several times asked the lad if the things were his own.—John Tetlock, aged 16, a scavenger in the employ of the Corporation, living at 84, Chorley-street, stated that on the 24th inst. prisoner came up to him at a picture palace in Sankey-street, at which witness was working, and asked him whether he knew anybody who wanted to buy an overcoat for sixpence. Eventually witness bought it from him for threepenee.—Detective- Sergeant Reddington deposed to arresting pri- soner, who was wearing the stolen jacket, and who admitted the thefts.—Prisoner was com- mitted to take his trial at the ensuing Quarter Sessions at Liverpool. .

===== 0002601_19140919_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
MONDAY.

Before the Mayor, Alderman Peacock (in the chair), Aldermen Smethurst and Bolton, and Messrs. S. Wright, W. Furness, A. J. Jolley, C. Jackson Holmes, and Fairclough. In a Blanket—Patrick McLoughlin, a hawker, living at a lodging-house on Pig Hill, was charged with being drunk and disorderly in Horsemarket-street and with assaulting Annie Fairhurst, aged five •years.—The prisoner ap- peared in Court covered with a blanket, the Chief Constable explaining that McLoughlin had torn up his clothes and that it was not the intention of the authorities to provide him with another lot.—Prisoner pleaded guilty to the first offence, hilt not to the second.—The evi- dence as to the assault showed that prisoner hit the little girl on the arm and made bruises with a heavy walking-Stick. The child screamed and this attracted the attention of the mother, whom the prisoner also threatened to hit.— Prisoner, who had been eleven times before the Court, was sent to prison for six months with hard labour.

Father and Son.—John Mullin, forge labourer, 13, Dial-court, was charged with obstructing P.C. Harrison whilst in the execution of his duty ,The evidence showed that the officer had the prisoner's son in custody on a charge of being drunk and disorderly. The prisoner pulled the officers' coat and later tried to trip him up. He was Apprehended and the son got away. The Chief Constable suggested that pris- oner should be bound over. Mullen said he only asked the officer to release his son, as he wanted to go to his regiment. Prisoner was bound over for six 'months and ordered to pay the costs. During the hearing of this case the son, who was in cottrt, shouted out that he was to blame. —Edward Mullin, the son, a private in the South Lancashire Regiment, was then charged with being drunk and disorderly in -Horsemar- ket-street and with using abusive language. He pleaded not guilty. The Chief Constable said ' the Bench had heard prisoner shout from the back of the court that he was at fault and .yet he pleaded not guilty! P.C. Harrison said lie arrested the prisoner, who got away when his father was apprehended. He was later brought from his house and locked up. Prisoner denied that he was abusive and said that he went quietly to the Police Station. When he was ar- rested people in the vicinity shouted " Shame." The Chief Constable said he saw prisoner being taken to the Police Station. He was quiet, but there were three policemen there. Prisoner said he knew he had a bad name, but wanted to re- trieve the good one he had lost. The last time he was in prison he was released by order of the Home Secretary for conspicuous bravery. Pris- oner was remanded for 24 hours, the Chief Con- stable stating that he would make arrange- ments to hand him over to the military. All Fours.—Thomas Brown, of no settled place of abode, was charged with being drunk in Bridge-street on Saturday. The evidence showed that he was crawling on his hands and knees when found by the police.—Prisoner was fined ss. including costs. The Tale the Coppers Told.—James Broom- head, of no settled place of abode, was charged with begging in Sankey-street on Saturday.— Detective-Sergeant Clough said he saw prisoner stop several young men in Sankey-street. on Saturday night and later heard him ask for coppers for his night's lodging, saying he had walked from Wigan. When searched he had Sc. 9d. in his possession, made up of 3s. in silver in a small poaket in his vest, and 2s. 6d. in coppers and a threepennybit in his trousers pocket.—Prisoner said he was only asking the young men in the street where certain works were situated, as he was looking for employ- ment.—The Chief Constable said the -coppers told their own tale; they would not find all that copper in a church collection. (Laughter.)— Prisoner was fined 55., including costs. Mistaken.—William Higginbottom, residing at 43, Nicholson-street, was charged with being 'drunk and disorderly in Nicholson-street on Sunday morning.—The evidence showed that a police officer going down the street early on Sunday morning saw prisoner outside the door waiting to he admitted. The officer went to the back of the houses, as all the gas lamps were out, and when he returned prisoner said he had no right to go there. The officer requested him to go in, whereupon prisoner became disorderly. —Sergt. Woodland, who was at tht Police Sta- tion when prisoner was brought in, said the man was very drunk and sang at the top of his voice when in the cells. There was no doing any good with him. He asked witness, who was in uniform, to feel his pulse, thinking he was the doctor. (Laughter.)—Prisoner was allowed to go, on payment of costs. TUESDAY.

Before Councillor H. Roberts (in the chair), Messrs. C. Jackson Holmes, J. Rymer Young, and J. Hepherd. Withdrawn.—The charge of drunkenness and disorderly behaviour 'preferred agilinst Edward Mullin, a private in the South Lancashire R.egi- meat, who was remanded the previous day, was withdrawn on the application of the Chief Con- stable.
