THE

EXAMIN

ER, SATURDAYS SEPTEMBER 19,1914.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0161.txt | included=true | included =====
PROGRESS OF THE WAR.

Summary of, Authentic 'Yews From All

Involved Areas.

SPECIALLY COMPILED FOR 'THE LAMM."

SEVENTH

WEEK.

DISORDERLY RETREAT.

THE ENEMY PRESSED.

Large Capture of German Guns.

On Saturday the Press Bureau published a summary of the operations of the British Ex- peditionary Force and the French Armies, but as this was expanded later we need not record it here. 'Phe fuller account will be found under the head "Our Share in the Rout." It was officially stated that the Germans had made a disorderly retreat

The Third French Army reported that it has captured the entire artillery of a hostile army corps—a capture which probably represents about 160 guns. The enemy is thus in retreat along the whole line west of the Meuse and has suffered gravely in "moral," besides encounter- ing heavy losses in personnel and material.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0162.txt | included=true | included =====
BATTLE OF THE MARNE.

ALLIES TURN FIERCELY UPON THE GERMANS,

Who Speed North.

The rout to which the Anglo-French forces on the Marne put the German right wing last week, and to which we referred in last week's "Examiner," was confirmed by a French official communication published on Saturday morning. During the present week the remainder of the German Armies have retreated as a matter of necessity, as their advanced right wing was thrown back. The French communique gave us information that had hitherto been lacking—the disposition of the German Armies. On the right —that is against the French left with whom the English are acting—is General von Kluck, and next to him General von Bulow. The Saxon army and part of the army commanded by the Prince of Wurtemberg form the centre of the enemy. Their left wing is held by the rest of the Prince of Wurtemberg's army and that of the Crown Prince. _

The extreme French left operating from the north of the Marne and the west of the Ourcq, in conjunction with the British forces march- ing from the south of the Marne, forced von Kluck and von Bulow backwards about 40 miles to the east in four days, driving them in upon their centre. The Germans by repeated violent attacks tried to break the Allied centre at Vitry-le-Francois, but did not succeed.

BRAVERY OF OUR BRITISH ALLIES." Speaking of this battle of the Marne, the French official communique said From tbe beginning of this action the German right wing—the army commanded by General von Kluck—which on the 6th inst., had reached the district to the north of Pro'rins, was obliged to retire because of the danger of ,being en- veloped by us. By a series of clever and rapid movements this army was succeeding in escap- ing from the embrace with which it was threat- ened and was throwing itself with the greater part of its forces against our enveloping wing to the north of the Marne and the west of the Ouroq, but the French troops which were oper- ating in this region, powerfully aided by the bravery of our British Allies, inflicted consider- able losses on the enemy and gained the time necessary to allow our offensive to develop else- where. _ _

• In the meantime the Anglo-French troops which have been operating to the south of -the Marne have not ceased to maintain their offen- sive. Having set out, some of them from the district to the south of the forest of Crecy, others from the region to the north of Provins and the south of Esternay, they have debouched on the Marne to the north of Chateau-Thierry. Fierce engagements have occurred from the out- set in the district of La Ferte, Gaucher, Ester- nay, and Montmirail.

OTHER SUCCESSES. The left of the Army of General von Kluck as well as the Army of General von Bulow are falling back before our troops. It is in the region between the plateaux to the north of the Sezanne and Vitry-le-Francois that the most desperate fighting has occurred. In this dis- trict have been operating besides the left of the Army of General von Bulow, the Saxon Army, and a part of the Army commanded by the Duke of Wurtemberg. The Germans have tried, but unsuccessfully, to break our centre by repeated violent assaults. _ _

Our successes on the plateaux to the north of Sezanne have enabled us in our turn to assume the offensive, and in the course of last night the enemy broke off the engagement on the front between the marshes of Saint Gond and the Sommesons district in order to fall back in the region immediately to the west of Vitry-le-Fran- cois. On the Ornain, as also between the Argonne and the Meuse, where the armies of the Duke of Wurtemberg and the Crown Prince are operating, fighting is still going on with alternating advances and retreats. There has not, however, been any great change in the situation as a whole. _ _

Thus the first phase of the battle of the Marne I s turning out in favour of the Allied Armies, since the German right wing and centre are at present in retreat. On our right the situation remains without any notable change in the Vosges and before Nancy, which the Germans have tried to bom- bard with some long-range guns. The general situation has thus been com- pletely transformed during the last few days, both from the strategical and tactical point of view. Not only have our troops checked the Germans' march, which they thought victorious, but the enemy has fallen back before us at nearly every point.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
VICTORY ASSURED.

German Armies Rolled Back All Along

the Line.

What was at first cautiously regarded by the French General Staff as a success in the first stage of the great battle was announced on Sun- day by General Joffre as a victory of the Allies, which was more and more complete, with the enemy everywhere in retreat and everywhere abandoning prisoners, wounded, and materials of war.

The French troops in the region of Nancy, after 10 days of continuous fighting, repulsed the German attack. The assault made on Mon- day, September 7th, was superintended by the Kaiser himself. Luneville was retaken from the Germans on September 11th.

A VIGOROUS PURSUIT. PARIS, September 13th. The following official communique was issued this afternoon, 3 p.m. : On our left wing the enemy continues his re- treating movement. He has evacuated Amiens, and is falling back towards the east. Between Soissons and Reims the Germans have retired to the north of the Vesle. They:did not defend the Marne to the south-east of Reims.

In the centre the enemy, who has lost Revigny and Brabaut4le-Roi, remains in the south a the Argonne. On our right wing the hostile troops which were on the Meurthe are retreating beyond Saint-Die and.Luneville. . We have re-ocoupied Pont-a-Mousson. All subsequent :accounts of the German retire- ment from the banks of the Marne position I agree in speaking of the immense debris of men —wounded, captured and dead—of buns, tind of supplies, cast off by their armies as they recoiled before the Allies.

AN INCONTESTABLE VICTORY. The following order was issued by the French Commander-in-Chief : The battle which has been taking place for five days is finishing in an incontestable victory. The retreat of the let,, 2nd and 3rd German Armies is being accentuated before our left and our centre. .

The enemy's 4th Army, in its turn, is begin- ning to fall back to the north of Vitry and Serinaise.

Everywhere the enemy is leaving on the field numbers of wounded and quantities of muni- tions. On all hands prisoners are being taken. Our troops, as they gain ground, are finding proofs of the intensity of the struggle and of the extent of the means employed by the Ger- mans in attempting to resist our "elan." The vigorous resumption of the offensive has decided our success. 'Officers, non-commissioned officers, and men, you have all of you responded to my appeal and all of you have deseryed well of your country. (Signed) JOFFRE.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
Allies Force the Passage of the Aisne.

HOPES OF A DEBACLE.

Hopes of being able to cut off large sections of the retreating armies began to be entertained. In the neighbourhood of Seissons the Allies forced the passage of the Aisne on Sunday night, overcoming a resistance which had lasted all that day. Farther south the army of the German Crown Prince was also in retreat, and its headquarters had been moved back from St. Menehould to Montfaucon.

The precipitate nature of the German retire- ment was shown by the abandonment of defen- sive positions which they had prepared between Compiegne and Soissons, north of the Aisne, on their right wing, round Reims in their centre, and also in the Argonne district on their left. DEFENSIVE POSITIONS ABANDONED. Paws, Monday. A communication issued at three o'clock this afternoon says:— On our left wing the enemy had prepared to the north of the Aisne, between Compiegne and Soissons, a line of defence which they were obliged to abandon. The German detachments at_Amiens retired on Peronne and St. Quentin.

In the centre the Germans had also prepared a defensive position behind Rheims, but were unable to hold it. In the Argonne the Germans have fallen baek towards the north beyond the Forest of Belnoue and Triancourt.

On the right wing the German retreat is general from Nancy to the Vosges. Yesterday evening French territory had been completely evacuated in this direction.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0165.txt | included=true | included =====
STUBBORN FIGHT ON THE AISNE. From the Official Press Bureau.

Monday Evening. All day yesterday the enemy stubbornly dis- puted the passage of the Aisne by our troops, but in spite of the difficulty of forcing the river in the face of a strong opposition nearly all the crossings were secured by sunset. On our right and left the French troops were confronted with a similar task, in which, like ourselves, they were successful. Many more prisoners were taken. It is reported from the French head- quarters that the Crown Prince's Army has been driven back, and that he has moved his head- quarters from Ste. Menehould to Montfaucon. The forcing the passage of the Aisne by the British Army on Sunday in face of a determined resistance by the enemy, supported by heavy guns, is accounted in France a brilliant achieve- ment.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMANS AT BAY.

THE ENEMY SHOWS FIGIIT IN STRONG POSITIONS.

On Monday the Germans turned at bay, after having been chased vigorously. General von Kluck, with the right, was entrenched north of the Aisne, while the centre stretched behind Rheims and across Champagne towards Verdun. Rheims itself was in the Allies' hands.

..... —.— The line of the German rally followed in the main that of the Rivers Aisne and Suippe from the forests to the north of Compiegne to Vienne- la-Yille, a small town half-way up the western slopes of the Argonne Hills.

The Crown Prince's Army has extricated itself from a dangerous position. It turned to the only way of retreat that lay open, and made for the gap between the Argonne and the River Meuse. Perilous though this adventure was, it succeeded. The army held the line from Varennes to Consenvoye, which, says "The Imes," is out of the danger zone of Verdun and covers the retreat over the Meuse.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====
CROWN PRINCE DRIVEN BACK. From the Official Press Bureau.

Tuesday, 3.50 p.m. The enemy are still occupying strong positions to the north of the Aisne, and fighting is going on along the whole line. The Crown Prince's Army has been driven further back, and is now on the line of Varennes- Consenvoye-Ornes.

_ The Allied troops have occupied Rheims. Six hundred prisoners and twelve guns were captured yesterday by the corps on the u,ight of the British.

. - Ram has made the roads heavy, and las in- creased the difficulties of the German Army in their retreat.

COUNTER-ATTACKS DO NOT SUCCEED. The Press Bureau issued the following state- ment on Wednesday afternoon The general position along the Aisne continues to be favourable.

The enemy have delivered several counter- attacks, especially against the First Army Corps. These have been repulsed, and the Ger- mans have given way slightly before our troops and the French Armies on our right and left. The enemy's loss is very heavy. We have taken 200_ prisoners.

- A Paris official statement on Wednesday showed that the rearguard of the enemy, at- tacked by our pursuing forces, were compelled to make a stand. Reinforced by the main Ger- man Army they took up the defensive along the whole line, which had been strongly entrenched at different points. The front extended through the Noyon region, the plateaux north of Vic-sur- Aisne and of Soissons, the " massif " of Laon, the heights to the north of Rheims, and the line terminating to the north of Ville-sur- Tourbe, to the west of the Argonne ridge, and continued on the other side of the Argonne ridge by another line passing north of Varenne (which latter point had been abandoned by the enemy) and reaching the Meuse near Bois Forges, to the north of Verdun.

THE LAST STRONG POSITION. The strong position described by our Allies is the last of the great semi-circles of hills which lie from north-west to south-east across north-eastern France, having Paris as their approximate centre. Behind it is flat plain to the banks of the Meuse.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0168.txt | included=true | included =====
ROLL OF HONOUR.

KILLED, WOUNDED AND MISSING. The following names have appeared in the casualty lists during the week :

SOUTH LANCASHIRE REGIMENT.

NEWS OF MISSING OFFICER. The relatives of Second Lieutenant H. G. w. Irwin, of the South Lancashire Regiment, who was reported in a recent official list to be among the missing, have learned that he is wounded and -a prisoner at Valenciennes. A message from him states that he has been well treated by the Germans.

A WOUNDED PRISONER.

The relatives of Captain V. R. Tahourdin, of the Cheshire Regiment, who was reported mis- sing and afterwards "unofficially" a prisoner, have been informed that he is wounded and a prisoner with the enemy.

OFFICERS WOUNDED. Groves, Lieut. E. J. IN COSHAM HOSPITAL. Lake, 7328 Lance-Corporal. IN CAMBRIDGE HOSPITAL.

Davies, 9832 Private J. • IN CONNAUGHT HOSPITAL, ALDERSHOT. Davis, 8256 Private W. Smith, 6882 Private T. Stagg, 9453 Private E. C. , IN LINCOLN HOSPITAL. Lennan, 7952 Bandsman W. (sick). A GENERAL KILLED IN ACTION.

The casualty list issued on Tuesday showed that Brigadier General Findlay, Royal Artillery, had been killed in action.

Brigadier General Neil Douglas Findlay, C. 8., had commanded the First Division Royal Artil- lery (Aldershot command) since 1910. Son of the late Mr. Thomas Dunlop Findlay, of Easter- hill, Lanarkshire, he entered the Army in his 20th year. Captain in 1887, lie was promoted to be major nine years later, and he became lieutenant-colonel in 1900. He served in the Hazara Expedition of 1888, during which he was mentioned in despatches. In the South African War he took part in the relief of Ladysmith, and was three times mentioned in despatches. General Findlay was 55 years of age. He leaves a widow and two daughters.

IN PORTSMOUTH HOSPITAL. Devonport, 7443 Private G. IN BIRMINGHAM HOSPITAL. Beecroft, 7275 Corporal W. Hodkinson, 8881 Private G. Hollingsworth, 7739 Private J. Schofield, 7740 Private D.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0169.txt | included=true | included =====
RUSSIA STILL COMES.

GERMANY MAKES AN EFFORT TOO LATE.

Germany has rushed to the aid of East Prussia and to her allies, Austria, on the Russo-Austrian frontier, but the effort comes too late. She has forced the Russians back in East Prussia, but the campaign in that quarter is now of secondary importance, or no importance at all. In Austria the enemy has been crushed remorselessly. The Russian military authorities have reason to be- lieve that the Germans sent eastward 10 field corps, of which half have gone to East Prussia and half to Galicia. Their places on the wettern front are being taken by the Landwehr. This German action is considered suicidal. A GERMAN SUCCESS.

Russia official reports state that the German troops brought from the west concentrated on the River Alle, whence they began a movement to the east, moving in great columns across the Masurian Lakes. The Russian advanced troops fell back to the east, but held the enemy's ad- vance.

After defeating General Samsonoff at Osterode the Germans, strongly reinforced, turned against General Vennenkampf, whose troops are threat- ening Konigsberg. Facing due east they ad- vanced upon the Russians in such a way as to threaten their opponents with disaster. Hap- pily General Rennenkampf's covering detach- ments were able to delay the enemy long enough for his force to extricate itself and take up a new line.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0170.txt | included=true | included =====
AUSTRO-GERMAN ARMY BROKEN

Large Capture of Men and Guns.

"The Times" correspondent at Petrograd reports, under date September 12th : According to official information the Austro- German Krasnik Army has broken and is sur- rendering in lame bodies. When the message left headquarters the numbers so surcelderim; hal reached 300 officers and 30,000 men, with 400 guns. Cavalry acting in the rear of the eminy prevents any possibility of escape. An earlier message from the Headquarters Staff said : .

"Having taken by storm the fortified position of Opolie-'Purobin, our troops on September 10th pursued the enemy. Several of our units ad- vanced fighting over 30 versts (nearly 20 miles). Our cavalry is working in the enemy's rear. Tomashow has been taken ate!' a stubborn fight. Obstinate fighting continues between Rawaruska and the Dniester." A week before the Austrian extreme right was crushed at Lemberg. Strongly reinforced, the broken troops took up new positions in the rear, and for a time they were allowed to remain un- threatened and even to attempt an offensive. Meanwhile Russia turned her attention to her enemy's centre and left, 600,000 strong. Heavy fighting continued for many days, only to end in a brilliant Russian victory. Driven from their strongly entrenched line the enemy took to flight.

CAPTURE OF GERMAN GUNS. The Russian attack upon the Austrian Armies has been pushed with magnificent vigour and determination. The success of the Russians in crossing the lower San drove the Austrian Army defeated at Lemberg back upon the great for- tress of Premzysl. Meanwhile the Russian Army advancing from the north defeated an- other Austrian force, which appeared to be also retiring on Premzysl.

- --- According to Petrograd belief, the Russians have in the fighting since their occupation of Lemberg taken 400 guns and 100,000 prisoners. They have also killed or wounded 250,000 Aus- trians. Amongst the captured artillery are many German guns-36 taken near Turobin— considerable German forces having been in- volved in the Dual Monarchy's complete defeat.

===== 0002601_19140919_art0171.txt | included=true | included =====
SERVIA'S STROKE. FIRST GREAT VICTORY OF TIIF WAR.

[ The Press Bureau at Nish has issued a com- munication reviewing the military operations up to and including the battle of Jadar, for which it claims importance, "not only because it was the first great victory to be recorded in the tragedy now being played in Europe, but also because one of the armies of one of the greatest European Powers was crushingly defeated—the Army of a Power which had itself chosen the moment as being most propitious for the execu- tion of its plans, relying on the exhaustion of Servia after two wars, although victorious, and a struggle against an Albanian uprising." According to the communication, Austria- Hungary had thrown against Servia 16 divisions of the 48 forming its Army—that is to say, one- third of its total force, about 350,000 men. The hulk of this force, some 200,000 men, were col- lected on the weakest point in the frontier, the Save and the Drina, from Ljubova to Shabatz. The Austrian columns, which advanced in semi- circular formation, crossed the Save and Drina on August 12th. The Servian forces, which con- sisted of weak detachments, were compelled to fall back, but succeeded for three days in delay- ing the passage of the enemy through the valley of Jadar. Meanwhile the Servian main army concentrated and moved forward to meet the enemy.

AUSTRIAN LOSSES. On August 16th the Serviana assumed the offensive. having defeated the left wing of the

Austrians, the Servian Army turn their centre. After the battles of A11,4' 17th, 18th and 19th, the enemy Wa9 beaten and was pursued all along the Krupani to Tcher. The enormous I°3. enemy are due to its massed forinatij the disorder of its flight. The Aliso 10,000 killed, 6,000 on the Teller. wounded Austrians remained on the , The prisoners, not including the well,nn, ber 4,000. The number of 30,000 14",°""i trians is not a definite total. One fifty guns were captured, as well as ammunition and war stores.

COUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON. EDUCATION COMMITTEE. TO BUILDERS AND CONTRACTORS. The Sites and Buildings Committee is pre- pared to receive TENDERS for the ERECTION of a PUBLIC ELEMENTARY SCHOOL, to be known as the PARR-STREET COUNCIL SCHOOL. Bills of Quantities and Instructions to Builders and Contractors may be obtained on and after the 12th September, from the Educa- tion Office, on payment of One Guinea, which will be returned upon the receipt of a bona-fide Tender. The Tenders must be delivered at the Educa- tion Office in the envelope supplied (which must be securely sealed with wax), by not later than 12 o'clock noon on Friday, the 9th October, 1914. The Committee does not bind itself to accept the lowest or any Tender. J. MOORE MURRAY. M.Sc.. Secretary and Director. Education Office, Warrington, 11th September, 1914.
